Bouncing threads for infinitary and circular proofs Conference: ACM SIGPLAN Conference on Programming Languages; January 01–03, 2018; New York, NY, USA Note: with title note CCS: Software and its engineering General programming languages CCS: Social and professional topics History of programming languages
David Baelde, Amina Doumane, Denis Kuperberg, Alexis Saurin

Abstract
. We generalize the validity criterion for the infinitary proof system of the multiplicative additive linear logic with fixed points. Our criterion is designed to take into account axioms and cuts. We show that it is sound and enjoys the cut elimination property. We finally study its decidability properties, and prove that it is undecidable in general but becomes decidable under some restrictions.



1

1.
Introduction

Fixed point theory has proved to be a valuable tool in computer
science, in particular for reasoning formally about software systems.
It is pervasive in programming language semantics,
concurrency, automata theory and software verification techniques.

In the setting of fixed-point logics, infinitary (
ie.
non-wellfounded) and circular proof systems
(
Dax
et al. 2006
;
Janin and
Walukiewicz 1995
;
Santocanale 2002
;
Brotherston and
Simpson 2011
)
have received much
attention in recent years.
Such proof systems allow non-wellfounded proof trees and impose some global
validity condition in order to ensure soundness. Typically, it requires that
every infinite branch is supported by some
thread
tracing some
formula in a bottom-up manner and witnessing infinitely many progress points of
a coinductive property.

On the programming side of the Curry-Howard correspondence,
fixed-point formulas correspond to inductive and coinductive types:
one finds programming languages equipped with
(co)recursion constructs whose typing naturally reflects the Kozen-Park
(co)induction rules
(
Mendler 1991
;
Cave
et al. 2014
)
.
Writing programs in these systems may be difficult, as it involves
coming up with complex (co)invariants. These difficulties are only
partially lifted through the use of guarded
(co)recursion or sized types
(
Giménez 1998
;
Abel 2007
)

in Coq or Agda respectively.
Furthermore, (co)recursion involves a suspended computation which makes it
difficult to analyze the behavior of a program.
As an alternative, one could naturally consider infinitary (or circular)
programs, equipped with a global validity condition ensuring that
they behave well -- in particular that they are terminating, or productive
for inhabitants of coinductive types.
There is surprisingly little work following this approach
1
1

1

We note the work of Hyvernat
(
Hyvernat 2014
)
whose use of
size-change termination can be seen as a form of validity checking.
It would be interesting to
precisely compare it with our style of circular
proof systems.

, and
foundations are missing.

This lack of studies can be understood from the fact that the aforementioned
infinitary proof systems for fixed point logics are all cut-free; hence,
the role of the validity condition in (syntactic) cut-elimination remains
unclear from these works. This shortcoming has been addressed first by Santocanale
and Fortier: in
(
Fortier and
Santocanale 2013
)
they consider an infinitary sequent
calculus for purely additive logic, featuring cuts and an extended
notion of validity, and they show that cuts can be eliminated from valid
proofs – in that setting, cut-elimination is not terminating but productive,
and converges to a (valid) cut-free derivation.
A key insight of this work is that the same validity condition that ensures
soundness in infinitary proof systems also ensures the productivity of
cut-elimination.
The result has been generalized later to the multiplicative and additive linear
logic, at the cost of a more complex argument, by Baelde, Doumane and Saurin

(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
.
Through these syntactic cut-elimination results, infinitary proofs for
the multiplicative-additive
\mu
-calculus
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
are given a computational
content, which is an important first step towards an interesting Curry-Howard
correspondence for that logic.

Unfortunately, existing notions of validity impose a quite limited use of cuts
in non-wellfounded proofs and many proofs that could be accepted as valid
are rejected.
In particular, this prevents writing circular proofs in a compositional manner, as exemplified in the following (supported by Figure
1
):

Example of a valid and an invalid circular pre-proof.

Example 1.1.

Consider formulas
N=\mu X.1\oplus X
and
S=\nu Y.N\otimes Y
respectively encoding natural numbers and streams of natural numbers in
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
.
Figure
1
presents two circular derivation trees, in the two-sided version of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
sequent calculus
2
2

2

We follow this convention for the example in order to exhibit more clearly the computational interpretation, even though the rest of the paper will be developed in the one-sided sequent calculus which is more concise.
. The computational interpretation of left-hand derivation is that of a function from streams of nats to streams of nats which drops its elements in odd position, keeping half of its elements only
3
3

3

Notice that natural numbers are erasable and duplicable as inputs in
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
, hence the use of the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(WNat_{l})}}
admissible rule.
.
The rightmost proof has a slightly different computational interpretation: it drops one element every two but also increments the element it keeps in the output streams. This is achieved by using a cut with a proof precisely doing this increment (depicted in the box).
Although the leftmost proof is valid, the rightmost one is not for the validity condition introduced in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
: indeed, the cut that is introduced belongs to a cycle and as a result, no valid thread inhabits the infinite branch.

These examples correspond (at a somehow informal level) to the Coq coinductive terms

and
incdrop
of Fig.

Figure 2
.
Guarded and non-guarded examples of coinductive definitions.

The present paper contributes to a line of research aiming at
providing and analyzing the computational content of circular
and non-wellfounded proofs, and improving their compositionality
of circular proofs.
From the Curry-Howard perspective, considering more relaxed validity
criteria is an interesting and important challenge as it affords a more
flexible way in constructing
circular proofs that, through the lense of Curry-Howard, more
flexibility to write valid programs on coinductive types.

Indeed, while our previous cut-elimination result
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)

is a significant step it goes only half-way due to
strong restrictions on the use of cuts along non-wellfounded
branches (or cycles in proofs) as described above.
We introduce here a new validity condition for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
,
the infinitary proof system for multiplicative additive linear logic with fixed
points. Taking inspiration from Geometry of Interaction
(
Girard 1989
)
, this criterion generalizes the existing one by enriching the structure of threads and relaxing their
geometry: bouncing threads can leave the branch they validate and “bounce”
(
i.e.
change direction, moving upward but also downward along proof
branches) on axioms and cut rules.

We define a new
bouncing
validity condition in the multiplicative
fragment and show that the obtained proofs enjoy cut elimination and soundness.
This new validity condition is undecidable but can be decomposed into an
infinite hierarchy of decidable conditions, via a parameter called “height”. Moreover, it naturally extends
to a validity condition ensuring cut elimination for the multiplicative
and additive fragment.

The following example illustrates with a simpler example the intuitive idea
behind validation by bouncing threads: it is not valid according to straight threads since its only infinite branch conains no infinite thread at all. On the other hand, one can trace the unfolding of the coinductive by following formulas
\nu X.X
upwards and formulas
\mu X.X
downwards while changing directions and moving from a formula to its dual when entering axioms and cuts, as represented on the portion of the proof that is represented. Indeed, after reducing twice the cut in each repetition of the cycle,
it yields a cut-free proof which is validated by a straight thread, which can be viewed as the “straightened” version of the above mentioned
bouncing thread
:

\pi_{0}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=534.03932pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Cut)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 155.96068pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.83331pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=415.04608pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\nu)}$} ${\times}2$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=410.0207pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\mu)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 32.51035pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=65.0207pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Ax)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}

Summary of the contributions.

We introduce a new decidability criterion for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
preproofs, based on bouncing threads. We prove that it guarantees soundness and productivity of the cut-elimination process. The criterion is compatible with simple compositions with cuts, as shown in
example

1.1
. Contrarily to
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
, we also provide a treatment of axioms, which introduces substantial additional difficulties in the proof of soundness and cut-elimination.
We finally show that a parameter can be associated to our criterion (the “height” of the bouncing thread) and that every valid circular proofs is validated with a bounded height. moreover, we show that the criterion with fixed height is decidable in the multiplicative case, but that without bounding the height, the criterion becomes undecidable already in the multiplicative case.

Organization of the contributions.

In Section
2
we recall the basic definitions for the non-wellfounded proof system
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
.
We then define, in
section

3
the cut-elimination procedure. In
section

4
, we introduce our new bouncing validity
condition, and show in
section

5
that it guarantees soundness of the system and productivity of the cut-elimination procedure. We finally study in
section

6
the decidability of our criterion in the multiplicative case. Proofs and developments omitted due to space constraints can be found in appendix.

2.
The pre-proofs of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}

In this section we introduce the multiplicative additive
linear logic extended with least and greatest fixed point operators,
and a system of infinitary (pre-)proofs for that logic.

Definition 2.1.

Given infinite sets of
atoms

\mathcal{A}=\{a,b,\dots\}
and of
fixed-point variables

\mathcal{V}=\{X,Y,\dots\}
,

\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
-formulas
are built over the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{rrll}\hbox{}\hfill\varphi,\psi&::=&a\mathrel{|}a^{\bot}\quad a\in{\mathcal{A}},&\quad\text{(atoms)}\\
&\mathrel{|}&\mu X.\varphi\mathrel{|}\nu X.\varphi\mathrel{|}X\quad X\in{\mathcal{V}}&\quad\text{(fixed points)}\\
&\mathrel{|}&\bot\mathrel{|}\mathbf{1}\mathrel{|}\varphi\bindnasrepma\psi\mathrel{|}\varphi{\otimes}\psi&\quad\text{(multiplicatives)}\\
&\mathrel{|}&\mathbf{0}\mathrel{|}\top\mathrel{|}\varphi\oplus\psi\mathrel{|}\varphi\binampersand\psi.&\quad\text{(additives)}\end{array}

The connectives
\mu
and
\nu
bind the variable
X
in
\varphi
.
The formulas of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}

, the multiplicative fragment, are those
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
formulas which do not contain
\binampersand,\oplus,\top
nor
\mathbf{0}
(
ie.
restricting the grammar to the first two lines).

Definition 2.2 (Negation).

(\_)^{\bot}

is the involution on formulas satisfying:

\begin{array}[]{c}a^{\bot\bot}=a;\ X^{\bot}=X;\ (\nu X.\varphi)^{\bot}=\mu X.\varphi^{\bot};\\
\bot^{\bot}=\mathbf{1};\ (\varphi\bindnasrepma\psi)^{\bot}=\varphi^{\bot}{\otimes}\psi^{\bot};\ (\varphi\oplus\psi)^{\bot}=\varphi^{\bot}\binampersand\psi^{\bot}.\end{array}

Setting
X^{\bot}=X
would be incorrect when considering formulas with free
variables, but it yields the proper dualization for closed formulas,
e.g.
(\mu X.X)^{\bot}=\nu X.X
.
Note that, since negation is not a connective, our formulas enjoy the
positivity condition by construction: all fixed-point expressions are
monotonic.

There are several presentations of sequents in the literature: a sequent can
be defined as a set of formulas, a multiset of formulas, a list of formulas or
a set of named formulas. The first two presentations (sets and multisets of
formulas) are not suitable in a Curry-Howard perspective as they identify
proofs having completely different computational behaviours.
The last two presentations are the most used in the proofs-as-programs
framework. Considering sequents as lists of formulas requires a constant use
of the exchange rule, which is very heavy. In this paper, we made the choice
to work with sequents as sets of named formulas, also called
formula
occurrences
.
We recall next their formal definition, in the style of
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
.

A formula occurrence is a formula together with an address. In a
derivation, all the conclusion (and cut) formula occurrences will have
pairwise distinct addresses.
When a rule is applied to a formula occurrence, the
addresses of its sub-occurrences will be extended by
\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i}\}

(standing for left, right and inside respectively)
in order to record their provenance.
This is of great importance for our developments: our validity criterion
traces the evolution of formulas, and this evolution is completely explicit in
their addresses.

Example 2.3.

We show in the following an example of an application of the
\bindnasrepma
rule in the framework of sequents as sets, as multisets and as set of formula occurrences respectively:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\scalebox{.9}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 9.79855pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=50.45808pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi$}}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi$}}}}}}$}}&\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 4.30551pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=61.44417pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi,\varphi$}}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi$}}}}}}$}&\quad\scalebox{.9}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 1.67007pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=93.66817pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{l}},\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{r}}$}}\hbox{$\vdash(\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi)_{\alpha}$}}}}}}$}}\end{array}

In the first case, the two subformulas of
\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi
collapse into one formula, in the second framework we keep track of the multiplicity but we cannot distinguish between the formula coming from the right and the one coming from the left. In the framework of formula occurrences, we can do this thanks to the tags
\mathtt{l}
and
\mathtt{r}
in their addresses.

Definition 2.4.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}
be an infinite set of
atomic addresses
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}^{\perp}=\{\alpha^{\perp}\mid\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}\}
, and
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i}\}
.
An
address
is a word of the form
\alpha.w
, where
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}^{\perp}
and
w\in\Sigma^{*}
. Let us call
\mathit{Addr}
the set of addresses.
We say that
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
sub-address
of
\alpha

when
\alpha
is a prefix of
\alpha^{\prime}
,
written
\alpha\sqsubseteq\alpha^{\prime}
.
We say that
\alpha
and
\beta
are
disjoint
when

\alpha
and
\beta
are incomparable wrt.
{\sqsubseteq}
.

The intuition is that atomic addresses and their duals will be assigned to the conclusions and cut formulas, and all the addresses appearing in our proofs will be sub-addresses of these addresses.

Definition 2.5.

A
formula occurrence
, or simply
occurrence
,
is given by a formula
\varphi
and an address
\alpha
, and written

\varphi_{\alpha}
.
Occurrences will be denoted by
F
,
G
,
H
.
Occurrences are
disjoint
when their addresses
are.
The occurrences
\varphi_{\alpha}
and
\psi_{\beta}
are

structurally equivalent
,
written
\varphi_{\alpha}\equiv\psi_{\beta}
, if
\varphi=\psi
.

Note that the relation of sub-address is the inverse of the prefix relation.
This may seem surprising, but it is coherent with the sub-formula relation.
For instance, in
example

2.3
,
\varphi
is a sub-formula of
\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi
, but its address is
\alpha\mathtt{r}
while the address of
\varphi\bindnasrepma\varphi
is
\alpha
.

We now define the rules of linear logic with fixed points in the framework of sequents as sets of occurrences.
As seen in
example

2.3
, a rule will look at the structure of the
formula underlying an occurrence, decompose it following a standard
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
rule, then assign addresses to its subformulas in the obvious way. This necessitates always making the addresses explicit. In order to lighten
notation, we will have the syntax of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
operate directly on
occurrences:

Definition 2.6.

Logical connectives are lifted to operations on occurrences as:

•

For any
\star\in\{\bindnasrepma,{\otimes},\oplus,\binampersand\}
, if
F=\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{l}}
and
G=\psi_{\alpha\mathtt{r}}
then
F\star G=(\varphi\star\psi)_{\alpha}
.

•

For any
\sigma\in\{\mu,\nu\}
, if
F=\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{i}}
then

\sigma X.F=(\sigma X.\varphi)_{\alpha}
.

Definition 2.7.

We define a duality over
\mathit{Addr}
by setting
(\alpha.w)^{\perp}=\alpha^{\perp}.w
and
(\alpha^{\perp}.w)^{\perp}=\alpha.w
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{fresh}}
and
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.
We then define
(\varphi_{\alpha})^{\bot}=(\varphi^{\bot})_{\alpha^{\bot}}
,
and write
F\bot G
when
F^{\bot}=G
.
We define substitution over occurrences as follows:

(\varphi_{\alpha})[\psi_{\beta}/X]=(\varphi[\psi/X])_{\alpha}
.

\begin{array}[]{cccc|c|c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 4.86107pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=86.91911pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash F,G,\Gamma$}}\hbox{$\vdash F\bindnasrepma G,\Gamma$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 4.72226pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=39.72218pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bot)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma$}}\hbox{$\vdash\bot,\Gamma$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=104.90521pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 2.56946pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash F,\Gamma$}}\hbox{$\vdash G,\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash F\binampersand G,\Gamma$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 12.29167pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=24.58334pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\top)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\top,\Gamma$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=138.58543pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\nu)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 15.96591pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash G[\nu X.G/X],\Gamma$}}\hbox{$\vdash\nu X.G,\Gamma$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 2.31944pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=62.66924pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Ax)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$F\equiv G$}}\hbox{$\vdash F,G^{\perp}$}}}}}}$}\\[12.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.00005pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=111.64145pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\otimes)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash F,\Gamma$}}\hbox{$\vdash G,\Delta$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash F{\otimes}G,\Gamma,\Delta$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 5.93054pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=11.86108pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\mathbf{1})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\mathbf{1}$}}}}}}$}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.7312pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise \end{array}

Figure 3
.
Rules of the proof system
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}

We are now ready to introduce our infinitary sequent calculus.

Definition 2.8.

A
sequent
is a set of disjoint occurrences.
A
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proof
is a possibly infinite tree, coinductively
generated by the rules of Fig.
3
.
Given a sequent
s
in a pre-proof
\pi
, we denote by
\mathsf{premiss}(s)

the set of sequents which are premisses of the rule of conclusion
s

in
\pi
.
Rules other than
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Ax)}}
and
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Cut)}}
are called
logical rules
.
For every instance of one such rule
we call
principal occurrence
the occurrence in its conclusion
sequent that is decomposed to obtain the premisses.

The infinite derivations of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
may be quite complex trees,
possibly not even computable. In practical uses one would turn to sub-systems,
typically the fragment of circular pre-proofs

(
Fortier and
Santocanale 2013
;
Santocanale 2002
;
Brotherston and
Simpson 2011
;
Doumane
et al. 2016
)
.
In a nutshell, a circular derivation is an infinite derivation which
has only finitely many distinct sub-trees up to renaming of
addresses
(
Doumane 2017
)
.

Notation 1 (Two-sided notation).

While it is proof-theoretical-ly convenient to work with one-sided sequents
as in the previous definition, it is more illustrating for some examples,
especially when we shall aim at illustrating computational interpretation
of some proofs, to allow to use the usual two-sided sequent calculi.
In the following (and in the examples of the introduction), two sided
sequents may be used:

F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}\vdash\Gamma
should be read as

\vdash\Gamma,F_{1}^{\bot},\ldots,F_{n}^{\bot}
.

Regarding the labelling of inference rules, we allow ourselves two conventions: either the inference rules are written with the labels introduced in Fig.
3
or, as in the introductory example, we use their two-sided names, for instance
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\otimes_{l})}}
and
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\otimes_{r})}}
, in which case this is a notation for the corresponding rule in the one-sided sequent calculus, respectively
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}}
and
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\otimes)}}
in this case.

Example 2.9.

We show in Fig.
4
two examples of pre-proof,
\pi_{\mathsf{succ}}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{dup}}
. (The reader can check their validity and cut-elimination behaviours once those concepts will have been introduced in the following sections.)

Figure 4
.
Examples of pre-proofs
\pi_{\mathsf{succ}}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{dup}}
.

Pre-proofs are obviously
unsound: it is easy to derive the empty sequent. Hence, a validity
condition shall be required for a pre-proof to be called a proof.

3.
The cut elimination process

In this section we introduce the cut-elimination rules for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proofs. In general, the cut-elimination procedure is not productive.
However, we will show in Section
5
that when we restrict to valid pre-proofs (that will be defined in Section
4
), the process is productive and outputs a valid pre-proof.

3.1.
The multicut rule

In finitary proof theory, cut elimination may proceed by reducing topmost cuts. In the infinitary setting however, by non-wellfoundedness, there is no such thing, in general, as a topmost cut inference.
In
(
Fortier and
Santocanale 2013
;
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
, this issue is dealt with by reducing

bottom-most cuts
, and when encountering during the reduction a cut which is immediately above another one, instead of permuting two consecutive cuts, merging them into a new rule called

multicut
and noted
\mathsf{(mcut)}
. A multicut can be seen as a metarule to represent a finite tree of cuts.

We will also use this multicut approach
4
4

4

Note that there are various approach to cut-elimination in infinitary settings, for non-wellfounded derivations or for logics including an
\Omega
-rule, in paritcular Mints continuous cut-elimination
(
Mints 1978
)
.
, but we now have to deal with axiom/cut reductions. This leads us to enrich the structure of multicuts, by allowing those to perform a renaming.

A multicut is a rule written as:

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\Gamma_{1}\quad\dots\quad\vdash\Gamma_{n}
\vdash\Gamma

and comes with a function
\iota
which shows how the occurrences of the conclusion are
distributed over the premisses (modulo renaming), and a relation

\models

specifiying which occurrences are cut-connected.
Below is an example of a multicut rule: the function
\iota
is represented by the red lines, the relation bottom is represented by the blue ones.

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash F^{\prime}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},G
\vdash G^{\bot}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},H
\vdash H^{\bot}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},K
\vdash F\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},K

Precise definitions and more explanations are given in

section

A.1
. Later, if clear from the context, we omit to specify
\iota
and

\models

in the rule name.

Now, we add the multicut rule to our proof system in order to perform cut-elimination.

Definition 3.1.

We call
\mu\mathsf{MALL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
the infinitary proof system obtained from
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
by adding the multicut rule.

3.2.
Reduction rules and strategy

The reduction rules are the same as in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
;
Fortier and
Santocanale 2013
)
, adapting them in a straighforward way to account for the extra labellings
\iota,\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}
in multicut rules. We give examples of such reductions in this section.

There are two kinds of cut reductions:
external

ones that push the multicut deeper in the pre-proof (Example in
fig.

5
.a),
and
internal
ones, that keep the multicut at the same level, and are not productive (Example in
fig.

5
.b).
The rules in the first category are said to be
productive
, since they contribute to the output of the process. Intuitively, the cut-elimination process succeeds if infinitely many productive rules occur on each branch of the proof.
An exhaustive description of the
\mu\mathsf{MALL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
cut-reduction rules is given in

section

A.2
.

Figure 5
.
Examples of external and internal reduction rules.

We now describe a procedure to eliminate cuts from
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
proofs, using as an intermediary framework the system with multicuts.
We start by embedding
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
in
\mu\mathsf{MALL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
by adding a unary multicut at the root of the pre-proof, with the identity as
\iota
and
\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}=\emptyset
. We then apply internal and external reduction rules to this multicut.
We will require reduction sequences to be
fair
, in the sense that every redex is eventually fired.

We introduce in the following section the validity condition, that will guarantee productivity of this cut elimination process.

4.
Bouncing threads and pre-proof validity

We now formally introduce our bouncing threads and the corresponding
notion of validity for pre-proofs.
Given an alphabet
A
, we denote by
A^{\omega}
the set of infinite
words over
A
, and define
A^{\infty}
to be
A^{*}\cup A^{\omega}
.
We will make use of the letter
\lambda
to denote ordinals in
\omega+1
,
i.e. either
\omega
or a finite ordinal in
\mathbb{N}
.
For such an ordinal, recall that
1+\lambda=\lambda
iff
\lambda=\omega
.
Finally, we will make use of a special concatenation:
given
u=(u_{i})_{i\leq n<\omega}
and
v=(v_{i})_{i\in\lambda}

such that
u_{n}=v_{0}
, we define
u\odot v
as the standard concatenation
of
u
and
v
without its first element, i.e.
u\cdot(v_{i})_{i\in\lambda\setminus\{0\}}
.
For example
aba\odot aab=abaab
.

4.1.
Threads

We start with a naive notion of pre-thread,
defined as a sequence of pointed sequents (i.e. sequents with a marked formula) together with a direction:
a pre-thread follows occurrences in consecutive sequents,
travelling up- or downwards.

Definition 4.1.

A
pre-thread
is a sequence
(F_{i},s_{i},d_{i})_{i\in\lambda}
of tuples of a formula, a sequent and a direction, such that for all
i\in\lambda
,
F_{i}\in s_{i}
,
d_{i}\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}
and if
i+1\in\lambda
one of the following
clauses holds:

•

d_{i}=d_{i+1}={\uparrow}
,

s_{i+1}\in\mathsf{premiss}(s_{i})
, and
F_{i+1}\sqsubseteq F_{i}
;

•

d_{i}=d_{i+1}={\downarrow}
,

s_{i}\in\mathsf{premiss}(s_{i+1})
,
and
F_{i}\sqsubseteq F_{i+1}
;

•

d_{i}={\downarrow}
,
d_{i+1}={\uparrow}
,

s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
are the two premisses of the same cut rule,
and
F_{i}=F^{\bot}_{i+1}
;

•

d_{i}={\uparrow}
,
d_{i+1}={\downarrow}
and

s_{i}=s_{i+1}=\{F_{i},F_{i+1}\}
is the conclusion of an axiom rule (so that
F_{i}\equiv F^{\bot}_{i+1})
.

If
\lambda=n+1
is finite we call
F_{0}
and
F_{n}
the
endpoints

of the pre-thread.

Example 4.2.

Consider the formulas
\varphi=\nu X.X
,
F=\varphi_{\alpha}
,
F^{\prime}=\varphi_{\beta}
,
F^{\prime\prime}=\varphi_{\beta.\mathtt{i}}
where
\alpha
and
\beta
are disjoint addresses. Let
G,G^{\prime}
be two disjoint occurrences such that
G\equiv G^{\prime}
. In the following pre-proof, the red and blue lines are two pre-threads
5
5

5

… which respectively correspond to the following sequences:

t_{r}=(F\bindnasrepma G;\vdash F\bindnasrepma G;\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F\bindnasrepma G;\vdash F\bindnasrepma G,F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F;\vdash F,F^{\prime\perp};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime\perp};\vdash F,F^{\prime\perp};\downarrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\vdash F\bindnasrepma G,F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\downarrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime};\vdash F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime};\vdash F^{\prime},G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime\prime};\vdash F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)
and

t_{b}=(F\bindnasrepma G;\vdash F\bindnasrepma G;\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F\bindnasrepma G;\vdash F\bindnasrepma G,F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(G;\vdash G,G^{\prime\perp};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(G^{\prime\perp};\vdash G,G^{\prime\perp};\downarrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\vdash F\bindnasrepma G,F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp};\downarrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime};\vdash F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime};\vdash F^{\prime},G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)\cdot(F^{\prime\prime};\vdash F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime};\uparrow~\!)
.
:

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma,{\otimes})}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}F^{\prime},G^{\prime}

\vdash F^{\prime}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime}

\vdash F\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bindnasrepma G

We shall define threads as pre-threads satisfying a particular condition that will make them compatible with cut reduction, in the sense that they will have residuals after cut-elimination steps. In Example
4.2
, the red thread has no residual
if one performs a cut elimination step on
F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime}
, because it
comes from the right-hand subformula of
F^{\prime\perp}{\otimes}G^{\prime\perp}

and goes
to the left-hand subformula of
F^{\prime}\bindnasrepma G^{\prime}
. In contrast,
the blue thread can meaningfully be simplified to persist over cut elimination
steps: its residual is well-defined. Geometry of Interaction
(
Girard 1989
)
provides a formalization of
these notions, assigning weights to pre-threads and determining which weights
correspond to meaningful computations.
We follow this inspiration, adapting it to our framework.

Definition 4.3.

Let
t=(F_{i},s_{i},d_{i})_{i\in 1+\lambda}
be a pre-thread.
The
weight
of
t

is a word
(w_{i})_{i\in\lambda}\in\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i},\bar{\mathtt{l}},\bar{\mathtt{r}},\bar{\mathtt{i}},\mathsf{W},\mathsf{A},\mathsf{C}\}^{\infty}
, written
\mathsf{w}(t)
and defined as follows.
For every
i\in\lambda
one of the following clauses holds:

•

w_{i}=x
if
F_{i}=\varphi_{\alpha}
and
F_{i+1}=\psi_{\alpha x}

for
x\in\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i}\}
;

•

w_{i}=\bar{x}
if
F_{i}=\varphi_{\alpha x}
and
F_{i+1}=\psi_{\alpha}

for
x\in\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i}\}
;

•

w_{i}=\mathsf{A}

if
d_{i}={\uparrow}
and
d_{i+1}={\downarrow}

(corresponding to bouncing on an axiom rule);

•

w_{i}=\mathsf{C}

if
d_{i}={\downarrow}
and
d_{i+1}={\uparrow}

(corresponding to bouncing on a cut rule);

•

w_{i}=\mathsf{W}
if
F_{i}=F_{i+1}
.

The weight should be seen as a bracketed expression, where each symbol

x\in\{{\mathtt{l}},{\mathtt{r}},{\mathtt{i}}\}
is an opening bracket with matching closing
bracket
\bar{x}
.
When defining threads from pre-threads,
we will be particularly interested in the following classes of
well-bracketed words:

Definition 4.4.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{H}
be the set of words defined inductively as follows:

\mathcal{B}:=\mathsf{C}\ \mid\ \mathcal{B}\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathcal{B}\ \mid\ \bar{x}\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{W}^{*}x\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{H}:=\epsilon\ \mid\ \mathsf{A}\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathcal{B}

A (finite) pre-thread is called a
b
-path
if
\mathsf{w}(t)\in\mathcal{B}
.
It is called an
h
-path
if
\mathsf{w}(t)\in\mathcal{H}
.

The
b
-paths start downwards and end upwards: they consist
of a series of U-shapes centered around cuts, glued together by axioms.
The endpoints of
b
-paths are negations of each other (up to renaming).
The
h
-paths start and end going upwards, and their endpoints are
structurally equivalent (up to renaming).
Intuitively,
h
-paths will be simplified during cut elimination,
and eventually disappear completely.

Definition 4.5.

A pre-thread
t
is a thread when it can be written

\odot_{i\in 1+\lambda}(H_{i}\odot V_{i})
where for all
i\in 1+\lambda
:

•

\mathsf{w}(V_{i})\in\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i},\mathsf{W}\}^{\infty}

and it is non-empty if
i\neq\lambda
;

•

\mathsf{w}(H_{i})\in\mathcal{H}
and it is non-empty if
i\neq 0
.

Notice that such a decomposition is unique.
We call
(V_{i})_{i\in 1+\lambda}
the
visible part
of
t
,
and we denote it by
\mathsf{vp}(t)
,
and
(H_{i})_{i\in 1+\lambda}
its
hidden part

and we denote it by
\mathsf{hp}(t)
.
A thread is
stationary
when its visible part is a finite sequence (of finite words), or when there exists
k\in 1+\lambda

such that
\mathsf{w}(V_{i})\in\{\mathsf{W}\}^{\infty}
for all

k\leq i\in 1+\lambda
.

For instance if a pre-thread
t=(F_{i},s_{i},\uparrow)_{i\in\lambda_{t}}
of length
\lambda_{t}
goes only
upwards with
\mathsf{w}(t)\in\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i},\mathsf{W}\}^{\lambda_{t}}
, then the above decomposition is given by
\lambda=0
,
H_{0}=(F_{0},s_{0},\uparrow)
and
V_{0}=t
.

Let us consider the blue pre-thread of Example
4.2
. We can decompose it into
a visible part (plain line) and a hidden part (dashed line) as shown below:

\vdash F\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bindnasrepma G

The blue pre-thread is then indeed a thread. On the contrary,
the red pre-thread from
example

4.2
admits no such decomposition.

If we consider the sequence of formulas followed by a non-stationary thread
on its visible part, ignoring its hidden parts (which have equivalent
formulas on their endpoints), and skipping the steps in the visible parts
corresponding to
\mathsf{W}
weights, we obtain an infinite sequence of formulas
as in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
where each formula is an immediate subformula or an
unfolding of the previous formula.
It is then well known
(
Doumane 2017
)
that the formulas appearing infinitely often in
that sequence admit a minimum w.r.t. the subformula ordering. We call this
formula the
minimal formula of the thread
.

Definition 4.7.

A non-stationary thread is
valid
if its minimal
formula is a
\nu
-formula.

Consider for example the formula
F=\mu X.\nu Y.X
. The minimal formula obtained by unfolding
F
infinitely many times is
F
itself, a
\mu
-formula, so the corresponding thread would be invalid.

4.2.
Pre-proof validity: the multiplicative case

The previous notion of valid thread suggests a first extension of the
notion of valid proof based on straight threads
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
:
one might say that a branch
\beta
is valid when there is a valid bouncing thread
which meets
\beta
infinitely often,
and declare a pre-proof valid when all its branches are.
However, this notion of
weak validity
turns out to allow
unsound proofs, as shown next.

T:=\nu X.X
and

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;

\vdash T_{\alpha^{\perp}\mathtt{l}},F_{\alpha^{\perp}\mathtt{r}}

\vdash(T\bindnasrepma F)_{\alpha^{\perp}}

\vdash

A proper notion of validity must therefore be more constraining.
We shall consider the following one, which requires that the visible
part of the valid thread
t
is contained in the infinite branch
\beta
.

Definition 4.9.

Let
\pi
be a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
pre-proof. An infinite branch
\beta
of
\pi
is said to be
valid
if there is a valid thread
t
starting from one of its sequents, whose visible part is contained in this branch.
A
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
proof
is a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
pre-proof in which every infinite branch is valid.

Example 4.10.

We show below examples of valid and invalid pre-proofs:

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

The pre-proof on the left is valid: its infinite
branch is supported by the valid blue thread,
whose visible part belongs to the infinite branch.
The right pre-proof is not valid, because the red thread,
though valid, has a visible part that is not contained in
the infinite branch.

4.3.
Pre-proof validity: accomodating the additives

The previous definition of validity is too weak to ensure cut-elimination for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
,
which is not a strictly linear sequent calculus (as
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
is)
since commutation/external reductions for the
\mathsf{(\binampersand)}
connective induce the duplication of a subproof. As a result, the extension of the validity condition in Section
4.2
fails to ensure productivity and validity of cut-elimination as shown in figure
6
.(i).

Figure 6
.

(i) Pre-proof family
(\pi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
S=\mu Y.((\bot\binampersand\bot)\bindnasrepma Y),T=\nu X.X
. Note that we omit the occurrences and that
k
is a parameter fixing how many times the
\mu
rule (in red) should be applied to the sequent
\vdash\bot,T,T^{\perp}
. (ii) Result of applying (infinitary) cut-elimination to
\pi_{1}
. (iii) Example of a slice of
\pi_{k}
.

The result of cut-elimination on the proofs in the sequence
(\pi_{k})_{k\geq 0}
can be split into the following cases:

(i) from
\pi_{0}
, cut-elimination is productive and produces a valid cut-free proof;

(ii) from
\pi_{1}
, cut-elimination produces an
invalid
pre-proof (see Figure
6
.(ii)):
any infinite branch following only finitely many times the left back-edge is invalid;

(iii) from
\pi_{k}
, for
k\geq 2
it is not even productive.
Indeed, in these examples, each
\pi_{k}
contains exactly one infinite
branch which is supported by a thread on
T
bouncing on the left-most
axiom and this thread is valid.

To understand the problem, consider the first step of cut-reduction (from
\pi_{k}
, for any
k
):
it is a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Cut)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
commutation step, which copies the right-premiss of
the cut (
ie.
the
non-wellfounded part of the proof): after this step, the pre-proof contains
two infinite branches, but only one thread to validate them.
While the leftmost copy can be validated by the original thread, the
rightmost copy does not contain a residual of the original thread. Of course,
one might consider a thread originated in the cut inference, but that
will not suffice to ensure validity, nor productivity, as
\pi_{2}
examplifies: its rightmost branch produces bottom rule.

4.3.1.
Sliced proof system and its cut-reduction

This issue is solved by refining the criterion using slices
(
Girard 1987
;
Girard 2001
;
Hughes and van
Glabbeek 2005
;
Terui 2011
)
and requiring that there exists
a supporting thread not only for every infinite branch of the proof, but also for every infinite branch
of every persistent slice
of the pre-proof.
In linear logic, an additive slice is a subtree of a sequent proof obtained by removing, for any of its
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
inference, the subtree rooted in one of its premisses (see Appendix
A.6
for details and precise definitions).

Definition 4.11.

\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
is obtained by extending
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
with the following three inference rules:

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}

\vdash A,\Gamma
\vdash A\binampersand B,\Gamma

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}

\vdash B,\Gamma
\vdash A\binampersand B,\Gamma

{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}

\vdash\Gamma

Definition 4.12 (Additive slice).

Partially sliced
pre-proofs are the non-wellfounded

\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
pre-proofs.
A(n additive)
slice
is a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
-free,
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
-free,
\mu\mathsf{pSMALL}^{\infty}
-preproof.

To a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
sequent (pre-)proof, one can associate a set of slices
by keeping, for each
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
inference, only one of its premisses and replacing the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
with the corresponding inference in
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}},{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}}

by applying corecursively one of the following two reductions:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=544.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 145.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}~\longrightarrow~\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.27083pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$},\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.27083pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}

More precisely:

Definition 4.13 (Slicing of a pre-proof).

The set of
slices of
\pi
,
Sl(\pi)
, is defined corecursively by

Sl\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=544.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 145.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}\right)=\left\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.84926pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=39.44211pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi^{\prime}_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{i},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$},\begin{array}[]{c}\pi^{\prime}_{i}\in Sl(\pi_{i}),\\
i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}\right\}

(The other inferences are treated homomorphically.)

Example 4.14.

Fig.
6
.(iii) gives an example of a slice.

4.3.2.
Cut-reductions for sliced proofs

Cut-reduction rules for (partial) slices of
\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
extend those for

\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
with specific rules for sliced additives and
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
.
Indeed, one may have a problematic situation,
e.g.
when a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}}
shall interact
with a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{2})}}
: cut-elimination cannot be performed by relying on subproofs.

Definition 4.15 (Cut reductions for slices).

The sliced additive principal case is reduced as follows,
if
\{A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2}\}\in\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}
, with
r=(\mathsf{princ},\{A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2}\})
.

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}

\mathcal{C}
\pi_{i}
\vdash A^{\perp}_{i},\Gamma

\vdash A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},\Gamma

\pi^{\prime}_{j}
\vdash{A^{\prime}}_{j},\Gamma

\vdash{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2},\Delta

\vdash\Sigma

\underset{r}{\longrightarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 8.05553pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=16.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{${\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}&\text{if }i\neq j\\[12.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=525.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp^{\prime})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 164.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.73317pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.52545pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.90186pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=49.08505pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathcal{C}$}}\hbox{$\pi_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A^{\perp}_{i},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi^{\prime}_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A^{\prime}_{i},\Delta$}}}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}&\text{if }i=j\\
\text{where }\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}^{\prime}=\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}\cup\{\{A^{\perp}_{i},{A^{\prime}}_{i}\}\}&\end{array}\right.

\text{or}\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=525.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 164.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 7.56941pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=22.36107pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathcal{C}$}}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}\underset{r}{\longrightarrow}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 8.05553pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=16.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}\quad\text{with $r=(\mathsf{princ},{\footnotesize\maltese})$.}

Notions of
b
-paths and
\epsilon
-paths can be naturally extended to additive slices.

4.3.3.
Persistent slices

Persistent slices are introduced precisely as those in which no case of the above mismatch ever occurs:

Definition 4.16 (Persistent slice).

Given a slice
\pi
, a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
rule of principal formula
A_{1}\binampersand A_{2}

occurring in
\pi
is said to be

well-sliced
if
no
b
-path starting down from the
A_{1}\binampersand A_{2}
occurrence of
this sequent ends in a formula

A^{\perp}_{1}\oplus A^{\perp}_{2}
that is the principal formula for a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}}
inference with
i\neq j
.
A slice is
persistent
if all its
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
occurrences are well-sliced.

Example 4.17.

Pre-proof in Fig.
6
.(iii) is (obviously) a persistent slice.

The following two properties of persistent slices are the key for the cut-elimination property:

Proposition 4.18.

All reducts of a persistent slice are
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
-free, and therefore are slices.

Proposition 4.19 (Pull-back property).

If
\pi\rightarrow^{*}\pi^{\prime}
(resp.
\pi\rightarrow^{\omega}\pi^{\prime}
) and

S^{\prime}\in Sl(\pi^{\prime})
, then there is a

S\in Sl(\pi)

such that
S\rightarrow^{*}S^{\prime}
(resp.
S\rightarrow^{\omega}S^{\prime}
).

4.3.4.
Additive validity

Def
4.1
and
4.4
of (pre-)threads directly adapt to the additives – as they are not specific to
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
– and allow us to consider the following definition:

Definition 4.20.

A persistent slice is
valid
if it is valid in the multiplicative sense
6
6

6

That is, every infinite branch of the slice is visited by a valid thread having its visible part contained in the branch.
.
A
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proof
\pi
is
valid
if all its persistent slicings are valid.

Example 4.21.

\hskip 56.9055pt\pi_{\mathsf{filter}}=\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=531.43044pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Cut)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 158.56956pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 166.38893pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=454.74287pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\mu_{l})}$},{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\oplus_{1})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 235.25711pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 147.1859pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=389.3626pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\nu_{l})}$},{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\otimes_{l})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 153.4515pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 147.1859pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=395.6282pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(WBool_{l})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 147.1859pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=536.5485pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Cut)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright Example of an additive circular proof

The circular pre-proof of Fig.
7
is an example of a valid additive circular proof. It corresponds to the last program considered in the introduction.

5.
Cut elimination theorem for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}

In this section, we shall establish our central result:

Theorem 5.1.

Fair reduction sequences on
\mu\mathsf{MALL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
proofs produce cut-free
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
proofs.

For expository reasons, we focus on the multiplicative case here. The treatment of additives, while bringing new cases, is similar and can be found in Appendix
A.6
.

The proof follows the same lines as the proof of cut elimination for straight threads
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
.
We will only sketch it here and emphasize the new phenomena arising from the presence of axioms and bouncing threads. The full proof can be found in Appendix
A.3
.

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is in two parts.
We first prove that we cannot have a fair infinite reduction sequence made only of (unproductive) internal reductions.
Hence cut elimination is productive, i.e., reductions of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
proofs converge to cut-free
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
pre-proofs.
We then establish that the obtained pre-proof is a valid proof.
In this section, we will only show productivity, validity of the resulting proof is shown in a similar way.

To show productivity, we proceed by contradiction, assuming that there exists a fair infinite sequence of internal reductions from a given proof
\pi
of conclusion
\Gamma
. We will also assume w.l.o.g. that
\pi
has only one multicut at the root. Note that since we perform only internal reduction rules, and since the latter do not duplicate multicuts, there is only one multicut progressing in the proof during this sequence of reductions. In the following, we refer to it as “the” multicut.

5.1.
Trace of a reduction sequence

Let us start by introducing an important tool to analyse internal reduction sequences, called their
trace
. The trace of an internal reduction sequence is the set of sequents that occurred as a premise of the multicut rule during this reduction sequence. The conclusion sequent of the proof is additionally included in the trace.
By analyzing the reduction rules, it is easy to see that:

Proposition 5.2.

Given a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
proof
\pi
, the trace of
\pi
is a subtree (possibly with open leaves) of the original proof
\pi
: the trace of
\pi
is the proof tree
\pi
from which some branches may have been pruned and replaced by open leaves.

An example of a trace is shown below: sequents not in the trace are grayed.

\mathsf{(mcut)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(\nu)}

\mathsf{(\mu)}

\mathsf{(\mu)}

\vdots
\vdash\mu X.X_{\beta\mathtt{i}}

\vdash\mu X.X_{\beta}

\vdots
\vdash\mu X.X_{\beta^{\bot}\mathtt{i}\mathtt{i}},\mu X.X_{\gamma}

\vdash\mu X.X_{\beta^{\bot}\mathtt{i}},\mu X.X_{\gamma}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\vdots}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\vdash\nu X.X_{\gamma^{\bot}\mathtt{i}},\bot_{\alpha}}

\vdash\nu X.X_{\gamma^{\bot}},\bot_{\alpha}

\vdash\nu X.X_{\beta^{\bot}\mathtt{i}},\bot_{\alpha}

\vdash\nu X.X_{\beta^{\bot}},\bot_{\alpha}

\vdash\bot_{\alpha}

Before going further let us see how we will use the trace to get a contradiction:

•

We will define an extension of the proof system
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
, and show that it is sound with respect to a boolean semantics.

•

Then we will show that the trace can be seen as a proof of a false sequent in this extended proof system.

•

This contradicts soundness and concludes the proof.

5.2.
The trace is almost a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
proof

As said above, we will need to see the trace as a genuine proof. In fact, it is almost a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
proof since it is a subtree of the original proof
\pi
, but is not completely a proof for the following reasons:

•

The trace may have unjustified sequents:
this happens when a sequent
S
enters the multicut during the reduction
sequence but never gets reduced. It will then be part of the trace but
the subtree of
\pi
rooted in
S
will not. This is for instance the case of the sequent
\vdash\nu X.X_{\gamma^{\bot}\mathtt{i}},\bot_{\alpha}
in the example above.

•

There is another reason why the trace might not
be a proof: its infinite branches may not be valid. The infinite
branches of the trace are also infinite branches of the proof
\pi
, thus they
are supported by valid bouncing threads of
\pi
. However, since the threads are bouncing, they might leave the branch and thus not be included in the trace.

We will show later how to handle the first problem of unjustified sequents. As for the second problem, we show that this actually never happens:

Proposition 5.3.

Let
T
be the trace of a reduction sequence starting from a proof
\pi
,
and let
\beta
be an infinite branch of
T
. If
t
is a bouncing thread of
\pi

validating
\beta
, then
t
is also a bouncing thread of
T
.

This is one of the difficulties specific to the bouncing threads. This result is trivial with straight threads
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
, since threads belong to the branch they support.

5.3.
Truncated proof system

To see the trace as a proof, we need to overcome the problem of unjustified sequents. For that, we will embed the trace in a proof system extending
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
, called the
truncated proof system
.

This proof system is parameterized by a partial function
\tau:\mathit{Addr}\to\{\top,\mathbf{0}\}
(from addresses to the formulas
\top,\mathbf{0}
) called a
truncation
. To get a sound proof system, we impose a coherence condition on truncations: they should assign dual values to dual addresses.
The rules of the truncated proof system are the same as those of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
, with an extra rule
which allows to replace an occurrence by its image in
\tau
. Pre-proofs and the validity condition are defined in the same way as
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
.
The advantage of the truncated proof system is that it allows to close sequents easily: if the address of an occurrence of the sequent is mapped to
\top
by
\tau
, we can justify the sequent by a
\top
rule.

The boolean semantics can be extended in the presence of truncations in a natural way: the occurrences whose addresses are in the domain of the truncation obtain as a boolean value their image by
\tau
. The rest of the boolean values are propagated through the connectives in the usual way.

We show that the truncated proof system is sound for this semantics.
Note that
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
can be seen as a truncated proof system, where the truncation has empty domain. In this case the truncated boolean semantics coincides with the classical boolean semantics. Hence
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
is sound for the boolean semantics.

Theorem 5.4.

The proof system
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
is sound for the boolean semantics.

5.4.
Trace as a truncated proof

Let us see how to transform the trace into a proof in a truncated proof system. For this , we need to find a truncation
\tau
that can allow us to close every unjustified sequent. In other words, we need to find a strategy for selecting an occurrence in each unjustified sequent, to which we will assign
\top
by the truncation
\tau
. This strategy should define a coherent truncation in the sense that it should not assign
\top
to two dual occurrences.

In
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
, we have given such a strategy: we select the occurrence of the unjustified sequent which is principal in the proof
\pi
. The presence of axioms complicates the situation: If
F
is the occurrence that has been selected in an unjustified sequent, then its dual might appear in an axiom rule
\vdash F^{\perp},G
.
By coherence of the truncation, the address of
F^{\perp}
must have been assigned
\mathbf{0}
and the axiom rule cannot be soundly applied anymore. To justify the sequent
\vdash F^{\perp},G
, we need to assign
\top
to the address of
G
. Since the same can happen on the
G
side, we need to show that it remains possible to define
\tau
in a coherent way.

To get our desired contradiction, we need in addition for
\tau
to assign
\mathbf{0}
to the conclusion, thereby obtaining a proof of a false sequent. This needs to be done while still respecting the aforementioned constraints induced by axioms.

5.5.
Summary

To sum up, we have found a truncation
\tau
i) which assigns
\mathbf{0}
to the conclusion formula ii) for which the trace can be seen as a proof in the corresponding truncated proof system. Since the proof system is sound, we get a contradiction and this concludes the proof of productivity.

6.
Decidability properties of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}

6.1.
An operational approach to threads

In this section, we will explicate how threads can be recognized by a specific deterministic pushdown automaton reading only the weight of a pre-thread.
This will allow us to define the
height
of a thread and the notion of
constraint stack
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}
be the deterministic pushdown automaton described in Fig.
8
, on alphabet
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i},\bar{\mathtt{l}},\bar{\mathtt{r}},\bar{\mathtt{i}},\mathsf{A},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{W}\}
and stack alphabet
\Gamma=\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i},\bot\}
where
\bot
is the empty stack symbol.
The transitions are labelled “
(a,\gamma)\mid\tau
”, where
a\in\Sigma
is the input letter,
\gamma\in\Gamma
is the topmost stack symbol, and
\tau
is the action performed on the stack (no action if
\tau
is not specified). If no stack symbol is specified, the stack is left unchanged. Symbol
x
stands for an element in
\{\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{i}\}
. No acceptance condition is specified, meaning that any run is accepting. Only the absence of an available transition can cause the automaton to reject its input, for instance reading
\mathtt{l}
with topmost stack symbol
\mathtt{r}
in state
\uparrow
.
The transition marked with a double arrow corresponds to the visible part of the thread.

\uparrow
\downarrow
(x,\bot)
\begin{array}[]{c}(x,x)\mid\mathrm{pop}()\\
\mathsf{W}\end{array}
\mathsf{A}
\begin{array}[]{c}(\bar{x},\gamma)\mid\mathrm{push}(x)\\
W\end{array}
\mathsf{C}

Figure 8
.
The deterministic pushdown automaton
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}

Lemma 6.1.

Let
t
be a pre-thread. Then
t
is a thread if and only if
\mathsf{w}(t)
is accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}
.

Proof.

The constraints on the stack match the grammar from Def.
4.4
. ∎

The stack of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}
will be referred to as the
constraint stack
.

6.2.
Undecidability of bouncing validity

In this section, we sketch why the validity condition is already undecidable for

\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
. This will motivate the following section introducing decidable subcriteria constituting a hierarchy of criteria while exact definitions, encodings and proof of undecidability are postponed to Section
A.7.1
for readability.

To show undecidability, we reduce from the halting problem for Minsky Machines, i.e. two-counter machines (2CM) able to perform increment, decrement, and zero test on the counters. The halting problem for 2CM is known to be
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete
(
Minsky 1961
)
.
The proof is only sketched here, and some technicalities have been abstracted away for clarity purposes. See Section
A.7.1
for exact definitions and encodings.

We encode the halting problem of a 2CM
M
using a bouncing thread. The thread of interest will always follow a formula
F=\nu X.(X\bindnasrepma X)
when going upwards, and its dual
G=\mu X.(X{\otimes}X)
when going downwards. The idea is to use the constraint stack to encode the value of counters, and the position in the graph to encode the control state of the machine. The general shape of the main preproof
P
performing the desired reduction is represented Fig.
9
. Boldface formulas are those introduced in cuts, and grayed formulas are the ones that are not part of the thread of interest. We will also ignore addresses in this proof sketch, except those relevant to our encoding.

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\nu})}

\mathsf{(\mu,\bindnasrepma,{\otimes})}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}F}

\hbox to6.11pt{\vbox to6.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.05556pt\lower-3.47221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.05556pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}F
\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},F\bindnasrepma\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}

\pi_{R}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}

\pi_{M}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}

\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mathbf{G},\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}

\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
