\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}F

Figure 9
.
A sketch of the main preproof
P

We build
P
so that the only branch which is not clearly validated is the one going infinitely many times through the loop. A thread validating this branch (in blue in Fig.
9
) must go through the two cuts, and bounce on axioms in
\pi_{M}
and
\pi_{R}
.
The trajectory of this thread in
\pi_{M}
will simulate the run of
M
, and it will be allowed to exit
\pi_{M}
if and only if
M
terminates.

We now give an example of one of the simplest gadgets used to perform this simulation: the increment gadget on the first counter.
Consider a state
p
of the machine
M
, whose action is to increment the first counter and go to state
q
. Assume counter values
(n,m)
are encoded by a constraint stack
\mathbf{l}^{n}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{l}^{m}\mathbf{r}
, where
\mathbf{l}
(resp.
\mathbf{r}
) stands for a left (resp. right) constraint on the unfolding of
F
, i.e. a relative address
\mathtt{i}\mathtt{l}
(resp.
\mathtt{i}\mathtt{r}
). This means that to increment the first counter, we need to add a left constraint at the top of the stack.
This can be performed by the following gadget, where nodes labeled
(p)
and
(q)
encode the current control state:

\mathsf{(\mathrm{Acut})}

\mathsf{(\mu)}

\mathsf{({\otimes})}

\mathsf{(\infty)}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

(q)\vdash G,\mathbf{F},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}
\vdash G_{\mathbf{l}},F

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G_{\mathbf{r}},A}

\vdash G_{\mathbf{l}}{\otimes}G_{\mathbf{r}},F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

\vdash\mathbf{G},F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

(p)\vdash G,F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

Here
A
is an auxiliary formula
\nu X.(X\bindnasrepma X){\otimes}X
, that can be duplicated as required and used to build axiomless valid proofs, denoted by an
\infty
metarule. The rule (Acut) denotes a cut combined with a duplication of
A
.
A thread entering node
(p)
upwards with constraint stack
\mathbf{l}^{n}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{l}^{m}\mathbf{r}
will enter node
(q)
with constraint stack
\mathbf{l}^{n+1}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{l}^{m}\mathbf{r}
.

In order to fully simulate the run of
M
, we also need to design gadgets simulating increment on the second counter, as well as decrement and zero test on both counters.
The main difficulty lies in the tests performed by the machine: we want the thread to follow a conditional branching, depending on the value of the constraint stack. This can be done, but because of the linearity of the proof system, we cannot avoid leaving some extra constraints encoding the results of the tests. These “garbage constraints” will be collected by the thread on its path downwards in
\pi_{M}
, after the simulation of the machine is completed.
Since we want to finish with empty constraint, we need to erase these garbage constraints.
To do this, we add a second gadget
\pi_{R}
performing the computation in a dual way: garbage constraints are fed to the thread, which rewinds the computation while erasing these unwanted constraints. All gadgets in
\pi_{R}
are dual versions of those in
\pi_{M}
. This technique is reminiscent of the one used by Bennett
(
Bennett 1973
)
to prove Turing-completeness of reversible Turing machines, where a history of the computation is produced to guarantee reversibility, then this history is erased by rewinding the computation.

We can finally exit this detour with no constraint, and perform a visible
\nu
-unfolding on the main branch (in red in Fig.
9
), before looping back to the root of the proof.

The global pre-proof
P
will be a valid proof according to the criterion if and only if the machine
M
halts.

Notice that among the simplifications we made here for clarity of exposition, the auxiliary formula
A
needed in some gadgets has been removed from the main preproof
P
.

6.3.
A hierarchy of decidable validity conditions

In order to recover a decidable criterion, we will consider restrictions on the constraint stack of valid threads.

Definition 6.2.

If
t
is a thread, we define its
height

h(t)\in\omega+1
to be the supremum of the size of the stack of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}
along its run on
\mathsf{w}(t)
.

Definition 6.3.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. An infinite branch is
k
-valid
if there is a thread of height at most
k
validating it. A proof
P
is a
k
-proof
if every infinite branch of
P
is
k
-valid.

The two following theorems show that the height parameter
k
induces a hierarchy of decidable criteria, whose union matches the full validity criterion.

Theorem 6.4.

If
P
is a valid circular proof of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P
is a
k
-proof.

Theorem 6.5.

Given a circular pre-proof
P
of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
and an integer
k
, it is decidable whether
P
is a
k
-proof.

We now give a brief proof sketch to give an intuition on how to prove Theorems
6.4
and
6.5
. See Appendix for details.

Proof.

(Sketch)
We will use the fact that once a starting point for a thread has been chosen, the thread evolves deterministically along the proof tree until a visible event occur. We define the notion of
minimal shortcut
which is a part of a thread with no visible weight, bouncing on an axiom, and ending in the first point where the constraint stack is empty. It corresponds to an
\epsilon
-path.

By bounding the maximal height of the stack by
k
, we can detect loops or declare stack overflow, and we are able to compute the unique minimal shortcut (if it exists) for each starting point in the finite proof graph.
Now, checking validity of the proof can be done using an algorithm for straight threads
(
Doumane 2017
)
, allowing them to take these shortcuts.

Theorem
6.4
is obtained by taking the maximal height reached by all minimal shortcuts of the proof graph.
∎

Combining Theorems
6.4
and
6.5
, we obtain that validity of a circular pre-proof of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
is in
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
, i.e. recursively enumerable. Together with the reduction from Sec.
6.2
, we obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 6.6.

The problem of deciding whether a circular pre-proof of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
is a proof is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete.

7.
Conclusion

We have studied non-weelfounded and circular proofs of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
and defined an extended validity criterion for the pre-proofs of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
compared to previous work by Baelde, Doumane and Saurin
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
. We have shown that our criterion enjoys cut elimination and soundness, but reaches the barrier of undecidability: in the purely multiplicative fragment already, a parameter has to be bouned by an explicit value to make the criterion decidable.

For future work, we plan to investigate whether this decidability result still holds when adding the additive connectives.

We also want to extend these results to more relaxed criteria, for instance where the visible parts are only required to meet the validated branch infinitely often.

A less sequential variant of circular proofs is also currently developed by De and Saurin
(
De and Saurin 2019
)
, under the name
infinets
: the canoncity and absence of commutation rules of proof nets may have good properties with respect to cut-elimination and we expect that bouncing validity may be fruitful in their setting.

Finally, the present work is a first step in improving the compositionality of circular proofs. In addition to strengthening our cut-elimination result us mentioned above, we plan to investigate how one can import results from sized types
(
Abel 2007
)
or copattern
(
Abel
et al. 2013
)
approach which also have good proprerties with respect to compositionality and may be used in an infinitary scenario
(
Abel and Pientka 2016
;
Abel 2016
)
.

Appendix A
Appendices

A.1.
The multicut rule

A new phenomenon occurs in the presence of axioms. Consider for instance the following pre-proof where
F=\varphi_{\alpha}
and
G=\varphi_{\beta}
:

\mathsf{(mcut)}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\vdash F,G^{\perp}

\pi
\vdash G,\Gamma

\dots
\vdash F,\Gamma^{\prime}

In the finitary cut-elimination procedure, we would reduce this multicut to the derivation labelled
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut_{1})}}
below. Doing so, we have to perform a substitution on addresses
(denoted by
[\alpha/\beta]
) to relocate the subderivation
\pi

on the required occurrence. Another option, described by the derivation
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut_{2})}}
below, is to avoid the renaming by keeping a link explicitely in the multicut rule.

\mathsf{(mcut_{1})}

\pi[\beta/\alpha]
\vdash F,\Gamma

\dots
\vdash F,\Gamma^{\prime}

or

\mathsf{(mcut_{2})}

\pi
\vdash G\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Gamma

\dots
\vdash F\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate{}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Gamma^{\prime}

We choose the last option to avoid the
global renaming, which would complicate our technical development.

A multicut rule will now be written as:

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\Gamma_{1}\quad\dots\quad\vdash\Gamma_{n}
\vdash\Gamma

and comes with a function
\iota
which shows how the occurrences of the conclusion are
distributed over the premisses (modulo renaming), and a relation

\models

specifiying which occurrences are cut-connected. A precise definition of the multicut rule is given in the appendix.

Definition A.1.

Given sequents
s,s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
where
n>0

and such that
s_{i},s_{j}
are disjoint for all
i\neq j
,
a
multicut
of conclusion
s
and premisses

(s_{i})_{i\in[1;n]}
is given by an injection

\iota:s\mapsto\cup_{i\in[1;n]}s_{i}
and a
symmetric relation
\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}\subseteq(\cup_{i\in[1;n]}s_{i})^{2}
such that:

•

For all
F\in s
,
\iota(F)\equiv F
.

•

For all
F,G\in\cup_{i\in[1;n]}s_{i}
,

F\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}G
implies
F\equiv G^{\perp}
.

•

\mathrm{dom}(\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}})=(\cup_{i\in[1;n]}s_{i})\setminus\mathrm{im}(\iota)
.

•

Given two sequents
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
, we say that they are

\models

-connected
on the formula occurrences
F,G
when
F\in s_{i}
and
G\in s_{j}
such that
F\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}G
.
We say that they are

\models

-connected, and we write
s_{i}\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}s_{j}
,
when they are

\models

-connected on some
F,G
. The relation

\models

on sequents
must satisfy two conditions:

–

two sequents must be

\models

-connected on at most one pair of
occurrences
F,G
;

–

the graph of the relation

\models

must be connected and acyclic.

We write this multicut rule as:

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

s_{1}\quad\dots\quad s_{n}
s

A.2.
Cut elimination rules

We detail the rules of cut elimination introduced in
section

3
.

Definition A.2.

External reductions
are defined in
fig.

10
.
In the first external rule, the sets
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
are the
subsets of
\mathcal{C}
which are respectively connected to
\Delta
and
\Gamma

respectively.
More precisely,

\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}=&\{s\ |\ \exists s^{\prime},s\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}^{*}s^{\prime}\text{ and }s^{\prime}\text{ is $\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}$-connected to }\\
&\vdash\Delta,\Gamma,F\otimes G\text{ on an occurrence of }\Delta\},\end{array}

where
\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}^{*}
is the transitive closure of the relation

\models

on sequents.

\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
is defined similarly.

\begin{array}[]{clc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule In the first reduction (
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\otimes)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
) we require that

\iota(F{\otimes}G)=F^{\prime}{\otimes}G^{\prime}
and take
\iota^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime\prime}

that coincide with
\iota
on
\Sigma_{\Delta}
and
\Sigma_{\Gamma}

respectively, and such that
\iota^{\prime}(F)=F^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime\prime}(G)=G^{\prime}
.
In the other reductions
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
are similarly
constrained.

Figure 10
.
External reduction rules,
where
r=(\mathsf{ext},F)
and
F
is the principal occurrence.

Remark 1.

Note that the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\otimes)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
external reduction yields
multiple multicuts, though always on disjoint sub-trees.
Thus,
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
is stable by external reductions.

In external reductions, we pushed a multicut away from the root,
above a logical rule.
If we start with a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
pre-proof and apply a reduction sequence where
external rules are applied infinitely often to each multicut, we
will produce at the limit a cut-free proof.
This is the reason why we say that external reductions are

productive
. This is not the case for the internal reduction rules
given next.

Definition A.3.

Internal reductions
are the
principal
reductions
given in
fig.

11
together with the following two reductions:

•

the merge
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Cut)}}
reduction

\begin{array}[]{l}\scalebox{.9}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=525.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 164.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=120.24982pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(Cut)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 21.4722pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathcal{C}$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta,F$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,F^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta,\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}}\\[10.0pt]

where
r=(\mathsf{princ},\{F,F^{\prime\bot}\})
with
\{F,F^{\prime\bot}\}
the principal occurrences that have been reduced.

In internal reductions, the multicut remains at the root of the redex.
Thus, if a sequence of multicut reductions eventually involved only
internal reductions, it would not produce be productive.

The use of labels in reductions allows us to define in full details
our notion of reduction sequence and fairness.

Definition A.4.

A
reduction sequence
is a finite or infinite sequence

\sigma=(\pi_{i},r_{i})_{i\in 1+\lambda}
with
\lambda\in\omega+1
,
where the
\pi_{i}
are
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
pre-proofs, the
r_{i}
are labels
identifying multicut reduction rules and, for all
i\in\lambda
,

\pi_{i}\underset{r_{i}}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{i+1}
.
The sequence is
fair
if for all
i\in\lambda
and
r

such that
\pi_{i}\underset{r}{\longrightarrow}\pi^{\prime}
there is
some
j\in\lambda
such that
j\geq i
and
\pi_{j}\underset{r}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{j+1}
.

A.3.
Cut elimination for
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}

A.3.1.
Trace of a reduction sequence

If
\mathcal{R}
is a reduction sequence starting from
\pi
, we start by defining the
trace
of
\mathcal{R}
to be the subtree of
\pi
whose sequents occur in the reduction sequence as premisses of some multicut.
Note that each node of the trace corresponds to a well-formed inference:
indeed, if sequents
S
and
S^{\prime}
are premisses of a same inference (which must
thus be a tensor or cut) and
S
enters a multicut at some point in the
reduction sequence, then
S^{\prime}
must also enter a multicut – though not
necessarily the same one.
However, the trace may have unjustified sequents:
this happens when a sequents
S
enters the multicut during the reduction
sequence but never leaves it; it will then be part of the trace but
the subtree of
\pi
rooted in
S
will not.

The unjustified sequents of the trace are called its
border sequents
.
Note that a border sequent cannot be the conclusion of an axiom rule nor a
cut rule in the initial derivation
\pi
.
If this were the case, by fairness, it would have been
absorbed by an
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Ax)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
or a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Cut)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
reduction respectively.
This allows to define the
distinguished occurrence
of a border
sequent as the principal occurrence of the logical rule applied to the border
sequent in
\pi
.

There is another reason why the trace of a reduction sequence might not
be a proof: its infinite branches may not be valid. The infinite
branches of the trace are also infinite branches of the proof
\pi
, thus they
are supported by valid threads of
\pi
, but these threads might not be
included in the trace. We show that this actually never happens.

A.3.2.
The bouncing threads of the trace belong to the trace

This section is dedicated to proving the following theorem.

Proposition A.5.

Let
T
be the trace of a reduction sequence starting from a proof
\pi
,
and let
\beta
be an infinite branch of
T
. If
t
is a thread of
\pi

validating
\beta
, then
t
is also a thread of
T
.

We now introduce a
a useful technical tool called the
residual of a pre-thread
.

Definition A.6 (Residual of a pre-thread).

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a finite reduction path starting from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}

and let
t
be a pre-thread of
\pi
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the set of sequents of
\pi^{\prime}
.
We call the
residual of
t

after the reduction
\mathcal{R}
the pre-thread

t\cap\{(F,s,d)|s\in S^{\prime},d\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}\}
.

By definition, the length of the residual of
t
is smaller than the length
of
t
.

Proposition A.7.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a finite reduction path starting from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
,
let
T
be its trace.
Let
t
be a
b
-thread of
\pi
and
t^{\prime}
its residual after
\mathcal{R}
.
Then
t^{\prime}
is a
b
-thread.
Furthermore, if
t
is a
B
-path of
\pi
,
then
t^{\prime}
is a
B
-path of
T
,
and
t^{\prime}
has the same endpoints as
t
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider a single reduction step.
Most of the claims follow from a simple inspection of the reduction rules.
For the last one (i.e.
t
and
t^{\prime}
have the same endpoints)
we have to additionnally rule out the possibility that, if
s
and
s^{\prime}

are the endpoints of
t
,
s
gets reduced at some point of the
reduction while
s^{\prime}
does not: this could only happen if
s
(or
s^{\prime}
)
was part of an
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Ax)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
reduction, but that would mean that our

B
-path can be extended into an
h
-path (if the axiom is at the beginning
of the path) or the reverse of an
h
-path (if the axiom is at the end).
∎

Definition A.8.

A pre-thread
t
is a
B
-path of
\pi
if:

•

it is a maximal
b
-path of
\pi
,
i.e. there is no
b
-path of
\pi
which contains
t
as an infix;

•

it cannot be extended as an
h
-path or as the reverse of an

h
-path.

Intuitively, the second condition means that the path cannot be extended
by an axiom on either side, possibly after silent steps corresponding
to
\mathsf{W}
weights.

Lemma A.9.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\{\pi_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
be a reduction sequence and
let
T=Tr(\mathcal{R})
be its trace.
If
t
is a
B
-path of
T
, then there is an index
i
such that
the endpoints of
t
are mcut-connected in the multicut of
\pi_{i}
.

Proof.

As reductions are performed, the thread
t
is simplified into

residuals
. As long as these residuals remain non-empty, they are
still
b
-paths in their respective derivations, and they keep the same
endpoints because the only way to reduce one endpoint without the other
is through an
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Ax)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
reduction. Moreover, the length of
residuals only decreases. In fact, since
t
is in the trace,
it strictly decreases infinitely often. Thus, at some point, the two
endpoints of
t
are directly mcut-connected.
∎

Proof of
proposition

5.3
.

Let
t
be a thread validating the branch
\beta
in
\pi
. The visible part
of
t
belongs to
\beta
, hence its belongs to the trace
T
. One needs to
prove that the hidden part belongs also to the trace.

Suppose by contradiction that some

H=(F_{i},s_{i},d_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\in\mathsf{hp}(t)
leaves the trace.
Thus there is an index
j\leq n
such that
s_{j}
is a border sequent of the
trace. Take the maximal such
j
. If
d_{j}={\uparrow}
then, since the
hidden part
H
ends in a sequent of
\beta
,
there must be a position
k\in]j;n[
where
the path exits the subtree rooted in
s_{j}
to re-enter
\beta
: we would
then have
s_{k}=s_{j}
, contradicting the maximality of
j
.
Hence,
d_{j}={\downarrow}
.

Since
H
is a b-path, and
d_{j}=\downarrow
, then there is
k>j
such that

H[j,k]
is a
b
-path:
keeping with the intuition that
b
-paths are well-bracketed words,
the opening bracket at position
j
must have a corresponding closing
bracket at position
k
such that the word in between is well-bracketed,
thus a
b
-path.
By maximality of
j
,
H[j,k]
is in the trace.

Let
k
be maximal with this property. Note that
H[j,k]
is a maximal

b
-path in the trace, since
s_{j}
is a border sequent and
k

is chosen to be maximal. Let us show that
H[j,k]
cannot be extended to an

\epsilon
-path of the trace.
Suppose by contradiction that this is the case. Since
s_{j}
is a border
sequent,
H[j,k]
can be extended only on the right. Thus there is
l>k

such that
H[j,l]
is an
\epsilon
-path. Since
H[j,l]
starts with a
downward direction, and since
\epsilon
-paths have the same direction in
their endpoints, we have that
d_{l}={\downarrow}
.
Thus by the same reasoning as before, there is
m>l
such that
H[l,m]
is a

b
-path. Thus
H[j,m]
is a
b
-path, which contradicts the maximality of

k
.

We can now apply
lemma

A.9
to
H[j,k]
:
at some point of the reduction, the sequents
s_{j}
and
s_{k}

are mcut-connected through the occurrences
F_{j}
and
F_{k}
. Note that

s_{k+1}
belongs to
T
(by maximality of
j
)
and that
F_{k+1}
is a strict sub-occurrence of
F_{k}
(otherwise, this
would contradict the maximality of
k
).

Since
F_{k+1}
is in the trace,
this means that
F_{j}
has been reduced which is not possible since
s_{j}
is
in the border of the trace.
∎

In order to view a trace as a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
proof, we shall devise a way to
justify its border sequents. Intuitively, we will identify each distinguished
occurrence with the true constant
\top
. To achieve this formally,
we introduce a
truncated
proof system in the next section.
Before that, let us mention a key technical result, which builds on the
intuition that
b
-paths are simplified during cut elimination.

A.4.
Truncated proof system

The truncated proof system builds on a truncation that forces
a semantics on particular occurrences.

Definition A.10.

A
truncation

\tau
is a partial function from

\Sigma^{*}
to
\{\top,\mathbf{0}\}
such that:

•

For any
\alpha\in\Sigma^{*}
,
if
\alpha\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)
, then
\alpha^{\perp}\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)
and

\tau(\alpha)=\tau(\alpha^{\perp})^{\perp}
.

•

If
\alpha\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)
then for any
\beta\in\Sigma^{+}
,

\alpha.\beta\notin\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)
.

Definition A.11.

Given a truncation
\tau
, the infinitary
proof system
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}

is
obtained by taking all the rules of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
together with the following rule for
\top
:

\mathsf{(\top)}

\vdash\Gamma,\top_{\alpha}

with the following proviso.
The rules of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
only apply when the address of their principal occurrence is not in
the domain of
\tau
, otherwise the following rule has to be applied:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=90.06502pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\tau)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 7.41699pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\vdash\tau(\alpha)_{\alpha i},\Delta$}}\hbox{$\vdash\varphi_{\alpha},\Delta$}}}}}}$}\qquad\text{if }\alpha\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)

The notions of thread and validity are the same as in
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
.

As in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
we define a classical truth semantics for
our truncated proof system.
Truncated occurrences (i.e. whose address is in

\mathtt{Dom}(\tau)
) are assigned their value under
\tau
.
The semantics of a unit
\top
or
0
is itself.
Then this semantics is propagated to more complex formulas inductively,
interpreting
\bindnasrepma
as disjunction,
{\otimes}
as conjunction, and
\mu,\nu

as least and greatest fixed points respectively.The semantics of an occurrence
F
under a truncation
\tau

is noted
\llbracket F\rrbracket
.
We establish, in the same way as in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
that
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
is sound wrt. this semantics:

Proposition A.12.

If
\vdash\Gamma
is provable in
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
, then

\llbracket F\rrbracket=\top
for some
F\in\Gamma
.

Since
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
is a sub-system of
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
, we obtain as a corollary that

\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
is sound wrt. the boolean semantics.

A.5.
From traces to truncated proofs

Definition A.13.

Let
\pi
be a pre-proof.
We define the relations
\approx_{\pi}
and
\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}_{\pi}
as follows:

•

F\approx_{\pi}G
if there is an
h
-path in
\pi
from
F
to
G
,
or from
G
to
F
.

•

F\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}_{\pi}G
if there is a
b
-path in
\pi
between
F
and
G
.

The relations
\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}_{\pi}
and
\approx_{\pi}
are symmetric —
note that the reverse of a
b
-path from
F
to
G
is a
b
-path from

G
to
F
.
The relation
\approx_{\pi}
is reflexive.

Proposition A.14.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a reduction. The trace of
\mathcal{R}
cannot contain
an occurrence
F
and two distinguished occurrences
G
and
H

such that
F\raisebox{0.51114pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}_{T}G
and
F\approx_{T}H
.

Proof.

We proceed by contradiction.
Let
t_{2}
be the
b
-path from
G
to
F
, starting with a
\downarrow

direction and ending with
\uparrow
.
Let
t_{1}
be the path from
F
to
H
. It must be an
h
-path, starting
and ending with
\uparrow
. Indeed, the reverse of an
h
-path would reach

H
with a
\downarrow
which is absurd since
H
is a distinguished
occurrence of a border sequent.

Let
t
be the
b
-path obtained by gluing the path from
G
to
F

with the path from
F
to
H
.
This path is a
B
-path of
T
, since its endpoints are distinguished
formulas, so they are in the border of the trace.
By applying
lemma

A.9
, there is a point in the reduction
where the occurrences
G
and
H
are directly mcut-connected.

Since
G
and
H
are distinguished, they are principal occurrences of the
rules applied to their border sequents in
\pi
. Thus, considering the
point of the reduction where they are directly mcut-connected,
there is an internal redex on
G
and
H
. By fairness it is reduced,
which contradicts the fact that they are distinguished occurrences
of border sequents.
∎

By
proposition

A.14
we can define the truncation
and truncated proof associated to a trace.

Definition A.15.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a reduction sequence and
T
be its trace. The truncation
\tau
associated with
\mathcal{R}
is defined by setting:

•

\tau(F)=\mathbf{0}
if there is a distinguished occurrence
G
such that
F\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}_{T}G
,

•

\tau(F)=\top
if there is a distinguished occurrence
G
such that
G\approx_{T}F
.

Definition A.16.

Let
T
be the trace of a infinite internal reduction sequence starting from
\pi
, and let
\tau
be the truncation associated to this reduction. The
truncated proof

\pi_{\tau}
is obtained from
T
by replacing every border sequent
\vdash\varphi_{\alpha},\Gamma
, whose distinguished occurrence is
\varphi_{\alpha}
by the following derivation:

\mathsf{(\tau)}

\mathsf{(\top)}

\vdash\top_{\alpha.i},\Gamma

\vdash\varphi_{\alpha},\Gamma

It is now easy to establish productivity of cut elimination.

Proposition A.17.

Any fair reduction sequence produces a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
pre-proof.

Proof.

By contradiction, consider a fair infinite sequence of internal multicut
reductions starting from
\pi
. Let
\pi_{\tau}
be the truncated proof of
its trace.
Since no external reduction occurs,
it means that an occurrence
F
in the conclusion of
\pi_{\tau}
can only
be principal in an
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Ax)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(mcut)}}
reduction of the considered sequence.
If
\iota
is the injection associated to the multicut after that reduction,
the same observation holds for
\iota(F)
, and so on.
In short, any occurrence
F^{\prime}\approx_{\pi_{\tau}}F

will never be principal in a logical rule. Hence we can replace all
these occurrences by occurrences of
\bot
.
We thus obtain a proof of the sequent
\vdash\bot,\ldots,\bot
which
contradicts the soundness of
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
.
∎

A.5.1.
Proof of cut elimination

We have shown in
proposition

A.17
that multicut reduction is productive.
To establish cut-elimination (
theorem

5.1
), it only remains to prove that
the resulting (cut-free) pre-proof is actually a valid proof.

Proof of
theorem

5.1
.

Let
\pi
be a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
proof of conclusion
\vdash A
,
and
\pi^{\prime}
the cut-free pre-proof obtained by
proposition

A.17
,
i.e., the limit of the multicut reduction process.
Any branch of
\pi^{\prime}
corresponds to a multicut reduction path.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\pi^{\prime}
is invalid.
It must thus have an invalid infinite branch
\beta=(s_{i})_{i\in\omega}
, corresponding
to an infinite reduction path
\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\tau
and
\theta
be the associated truncation and
truncated proof in
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
.

We set
\mathsf{Froz}(\beta)
to be the set of occurrences of
\beta

which are never principal. For convenience we will use the weakening rule:

\mathsf{(W)}

\vdash\Gamma
\vdash\Gamma,\Delta

Weakening is indeed admissible as long as the derivation on which it
is applied contains an infinite branch,
since one can let the weakened occurrences "travel" into this infinite branch.
Without loss of generality, we now assume that all occurrences of

\mathsf{Froz}(\beta)
have been weakened away in
\beta
.

We define the truncation
\tau^{\prime}
to be the truncation obtained by extending
\tau
as follows.
For every occurrence
F_{1}\bindnasrepma F_{2}
which is principal in
\beta
, we set, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\tau^{\prime}(F_{i})=\mathbf{0}
if
F_{i}\in\mathsf{Froz}(\beta)
.

Let
\beta_{\geq i}
be the suffix of
\beta
starting from the
i^{th}
element.
If
\vdash\Gamma
is the conclusion of
\beta_{\geq i}
, then for every
\Delta\subseteq\Gamma
, we define coinductively the proof
{\beta_{\geq i}}^{\bot}(\Delta)
of conclusion
\vdash\Delta^{\bot}
as follows. We proceed by case analysis on the rule applied to the conclusion of
\beta
. This rule can be either a logical rule or a weakening.
If the rule is a weakening, then it is simulated by a weakening rule.
If it is a logical rule, then let
F
be its principal occurrence. We set
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta\setminus\{F\}
. We have either:

•

F=\sigma X.G
where
\sigma\in\{\mu,\nu\}
. We set
\overline{\sigma}
to be the dual of
\sigma
, and:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 3.08307pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=408.52148pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize($\overline{\sigma}$)}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 21.31291pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=172.75186pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 3.08307pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime},G[F/X])$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},G^{\bot}[F^{\bot}/X]$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

•

F=G\otimes H
. Suppose wlog. that
G\in s_{i+1}
(i.e.,
H
left the branch
\beta
). We set
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}\cap s_{i+1}
and:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=400.47285pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\bindnasrepma)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 10.18121pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 3.38214pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=410.65407pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(W)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=112.15477pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime\prime},G)$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\prime\bot},G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},G^{\bot},H^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

•

F=G\bindnasrepma H
and
G\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and
H\notin\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
,
or symmetrically. We set:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=540.14583pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\otimes)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 149.85419pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=455.06862pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.75066pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=23.50133pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\tau^{\prime})}$}, {\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\top)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\vdash G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime},H)$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},H^{\bot}$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

•

F=G\bindnasrepma H
,
G\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and
H\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
. In this case we set:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=540.14583pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\otimes)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 149.85419pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 23.29857pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=391.59715pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\tau^{\prime})}$}, {\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\top)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.75066pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=23.50133pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\tau^{\prime})}$}, {\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\top)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\vdash G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},H^{\bot}$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

•

F=G\bindnasrepma H
,
G\notin\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and
H\notin\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
. In this case we set:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 8.74979pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=535.77094pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\otimes)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 154.22908pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=455.06862pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=73.252pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 8.74979pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(G)$}}\hbox{$\vdash G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime},H)$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},H^{\bot}$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

Observe now that

\beta^{\bot}:=\beta_{\geq 0}^{\bot}
is a proof in the truncated proof system

\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}_{\tau^{\prime}}
of conclusion
A^{\bot}
. Indeed, its threads (which
are necessarily straight threads since no cuts and axioms are involved)
are the duals of the threads of the branch
\beta
, which are by hypothesis
not valid.

Since
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}_{\tau^{\prime}}
is sound, we have that
\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket=\top
. But the
truncated proof
\theta
is a
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}_{\tau}
proof of conclusion
A
, and again by soundness
we have that
\llbracket A\rrbracket=\top
: contradiction.
∎

A.6.
Extending
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\infty}
cut-elimination to the additives

In this appendix, we give details on the proof of cut-elimination theorem for full
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
. First, we provide precise definition for the sliced proof system and the associated partial cut-reduction relation and the introduce persistent slices. We can then formulate precisely the additive validity criterion and prove the cut-elimination theorem.
The proof schema of additive cut-elimination follows the same pattern as in the multiplicative case but we check that the multiplicative result can indeed be lifted. Most of the definitions can be very straightforwardly lifted to the additive but for the soundness result some work has to be done: we show that we do not need a full soundness result but that a soundness result wrt. a specific class of derivations, called
\tau
-adapted proofs, which then allows us to prove productivity of cut-elimination and preservation of validity by fair-reduction sequences.

A.6.1.
Sliced proof system and its cut-reduction

To solve the previous issue, we will make use of slices, originally introduced by Girard in his seminal paper and later used in the analysis of interaction and cut-elimination of linear logic in the setting of Ludics
(
Girard 2001
;
Terui 2011
)
or in the design of additive proof-nets
(
Hughes and van
Glabbeek 2005
)
.

Definition A.18 (Additive slice).

A
sliced pre-proof
is a pre-proof built on a variant of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
,
\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
, where the inference rule
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
has been replaced by the following two rules:

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}

\vdash A,\Gamma
\vdash A\binampersand B,\Gamma

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}

\vdash B,\Gamma
\vdash A\binampersand B,\Gamma

Definition A.19 (Slicing of a pre-proof).

To a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
sequent (pre-)proof, one can associate a set of slices
by keeping, for each
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
inference, only one of its premisses and replacing the
\binampersand
by the corresponding inference in
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}},{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}}
. More precisely, a
slice of
\pi
is any
\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
derivation obtained from
\pi
by applying corecursively one of the following two reductions (the other inferences are treated homomorphically):

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=544.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 145.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}~\longrightarrow~\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.27083pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$},\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.27083pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}

that is:

Sl\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=544.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 145.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=41.7558pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi_{1}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi_{2}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$}\right)=\left\{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.84926pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=399.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 10.6915pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=39.44211pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi^{\prime}_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{i},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash A_{1}\binampersand A_{2},\Gamma$}}}}}}$},\begin{array}[]{c}\pi^{\prime}_{i}\in Sl(\pi_{i}),\\
i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}\right\}

A.6.2.
Cut-reductions for sliced proofs

Cut-reduction rules for slices of
\mu\mathsf{SMALL}^{\infty}
are identical to
those for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
except for the sliced additives. In this case,
one may have a problematic situation when a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}}
shall interact
with a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{2})}}
: cut-elimination cannot be performed.
Among the several ways to cope with this problem, we choose here to
introduce a special inference,
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
, a generalized axiom rule
allowing to derive any sequent,
which denotes the fact that a bad interaction occurred.

{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}

\vdash\Gamma

This does not impact the technical development since this serves essentially the purpose of defining those slices which avoid the mismatch.

Considering the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
inference, cut-reductions for slices are specified as follows
7
7

7

This reduction is straightforwardly extended to multicuts and some care shall be taken in treating
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
, in particular any cut involving
\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}
is reduced to
\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}
itself as standard in ludics.
:

Definition A.20 (Cut reductions for slices).

The sliced additive principal case is reduced as follows,
if
\{A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2}\}\in\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}
, with
r=(\mathsf{princ},\{A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2}\})
.

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}

\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}

\mathcal{C}
\pi_{i}
\vdash A^{\perp}_{i},\Gamma

\vdash A^{\perp}_{1}\binampersand A^{\perp}_{2},\Gamma

\pi^{\prime}_{j}
\vdash{A^{\prime}}_{j},\Gamma

\vdash{A^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus{A^{\prime}}_{2},\Delta

\vdash\Sigma

\underset{r}{\longrightarrow}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 8.05553pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=16.11105pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{${\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}&\text{if }i\neq j\\[12.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=525.55553pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp^{\prime})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 164.44447pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.73317pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=386.52545pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 11.90186pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=49.08505pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathcal{C}$}}\hbox{$\pi_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A^{\perp}_{i},\Gamma$}}}}\hbox{$\pi^{\prime}_{i}$}}\hbox{$\vdash A^{\prime}_{i},\Delta$}}}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Sigma$}}}}}}$}&\text{if }i=j\\
\text{where }\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}^{\prime}=\raisebox{0.50003pt}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\models$}}\cup\{\{A^{\perp}_{i},{A^{\prime}}_{i}\}\}&\end{array}\right.

Notions of
b
-paths and
\epsilon
-paths can be naturally extended to additive slices.

A.6.3.
Persistent slices

To state the validity criterion for the additives, one needs to describe
persistent
slices that will never produce a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
:

Definition A.21 (Persistent slice).

Given a slice
\pi
, a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
rule of principal formula
A_{1}\binampersand A_{2}

occurring in
\pi
is said to be

well-sliced
if
no
b
-path starting down from the
A_{1}\binampersand A_{2}
occurrence of
this sequent ends in a

A^{\perp}_{1}\oplus A^{\perp}_{2}
which principal formula of a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}}
inference with
i\neq j
.
A slice is
persistent
if all its
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
occurrences are well-sliced.

Lemma A.22.

In a persistent slice,
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
rules are characterized by the following property: (i) either there exists a
b
-path starting in
A_{i}
, (ii) or there is a maximal pre-thread
t
starting from
A_{i}
such that
\mathsf{w}(t)
is prefix of a word in
\mathcal{B}
ends in the conclusion sequent or in the conclusion of a
\top
rule (iii) or no such maximal pre-thread
t
(starting from
A_{i}
such that
\mathsf{w}(t)
is prefix of a word in
\mathcal{B}
) exists and they mutually extend into an infinite pre-thead.

Proof.

By case distinction, the fourth case being disabled by the condition of well-sliced
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{i})}}
rules.
∎

In establishing the cut-elimination result, an intermediate proof system will be useful, that of partially sliced
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proofs, in which both
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}},{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}}
and
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}}
occur:

The reader will notice that the additive
\binampersand
-inferences occurring in the trace of a reduction path will always be sliced inferences (
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{1})}}
or
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand_{2})}}
).

Proposition A.23.

All reducts of a persistent slice are
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
-free.

Proof.

The property relies on the simple observation that (i) any cut-redcution step from a persistent slice results in a persistent slice and (ii) if there is a reduction step from slice
S
which creates a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{({\footnotesize\maltese})}}
rule, then
S
is non-persistent.
∎

Proposition A.24 (Pull-back property).

If
\pi\rightarrow^{*}\pi^{\prime}
(resp.
\pi\rightarrow^{\omega}\pi^{\prime}
) and
S^{\prime}
is a slice of
\pi^{\prime}
, then there is a
slice
S
of
\pi
such that
S\rightarrow^{*}S^{\prime}
(resp.
S\rightarrow^{\omega}S^{\prime}
).

Proof.

In the case of the finitary reduction, this is a well-known property of slices.

For the infinite fair reductions, it results from the fact that fair reductions are necessary strongly convergent in the sense of infinitary rewriting and therefore one can find a point to which in the reduction in which any point of the resulting slice is being produced and trace it back. The obtained slice is of course persistent since it reduces to
S^{\prime}
.
∎

A.6.4.
Additive bouncing validity criterion

Definitions
4.1
and
4.4
of (pre-)threads directly adapt to the additives as they are not specific to the multiplicative fragment.

Definition A.25 (Validity).

A slicing is
valid
if it is persistent and if it is valid in the multiplicative sense
8
8

8

That is, every infinite branch of the slicing is visited by a valid thread having its visible part contained in the branch.
.
A
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proof
\pi
is
valid
if all its persistent slicings are valid.

A.6.5.
Additive cut-elimination theorem

We now state the cut-elimination theorem and
give a shema of the proofs.

Theorem A.26.

Fair infinite cut-reduction on
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
proofs is productive and produces valid proofs.

Schema of the proof

For cut-elimination, the proof goes by contradiction: assuming that we have a non productive fair cut-elimination, we may assume that it consists only of internal reduction steps and the trace of this cut-elimination is actually a slice with open premisses. It is actually contained in a persistent slice of
\pi
. As a consequence, there is an infinite branch of
\pi
which is entirely visited by the trace and this branch is visited by a thread thanks to additive validity.
By adapting the truncated proof system to the additives and proving a restricted soundness result, we transform the valid persistent slide in a truncated derivation of the empty sequent by pruning the conclusion formulas which are never principal in the trace, from which results the contradiction.

For proving validity, it goes also by contradiction: assume the produced cut-free proof of
\vdash F

\pi^{\prime}
contains a persistent slice
S^{\prime}
containing an invalid branch
\beta^{\prime}
.
By the pull-back property, we find a persistent slice
S
of
\pi
reducing to
S^{\prime}
which is valid by assumption.
From the invalid branch
\beta^{\prime}
one can build a cut-free proof
\beta^{\prime\perp}
of
\vdash F^{\perp}
together with a truncation
\tau^{\prime}
ensuring that
F
is interpreted a
0
while validity of S and adaptation wrt.
\tau^{\prime}
ensures that
F
is interpreted as
\top
, a contradiction.

We now establish productivity of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
cut-elimination:

Theorem A.27.

Fair reduction sequence of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
are productive.

To do so, first notice that the following notions of appendix
A.3
can be straightforwardly adapted to the additive case (or to additive slices):

•

Definition
A.6
and Lemma
A.9
adapt without change to
persistent slices
as it is not specific to the multiplicative case (but persistency is needed).

•

Proposition
5.3
applies to the trace of a persistent slice
\pi
.

•

Missing and unjustified sequents can be extended to reduction paths of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
pre-proofs (non-sliced) for the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
connective as done already in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
.

•

While truncations need no adaptation, the truncated semantics shall be adapted to the additives by adding the following clauses:

\begin{array}[]{l}\llbracket(\varphi\binampersand\psi)_{\alpha}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}}=\llbracket\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{l}}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\llbracket\psi_{\alpha\mathtt{r}}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}},\\[12.0pt]
\llbracket(\varphi\oplus\psi)_{\alpha}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}}=\llbracket\varphi_{\alpha\mathtt{l}}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}}\vee\llbracket\psi_{\alpha\mathtt{r}}\rrbracket^{\mathcal{E}}\end{array}

•

The truncated proof system is extended in the most natural way (as in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
).

•

Truncation induced by a reduction path is lifted to the additive case and it is well-defined since the additive inference would simply add a tricky case of
missing
sequent for a
\binampersand
premiss erased when reducing a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(Cut)}}/{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
cut but there cannot be an
\epsilon
-path in this case. Therefore the only case to treat is that of distinguished occurrences of unjustified sequents of type 1 which works as for the multiplicative.

As for adapting soundness (Prop.
A.12
), we actually
do not need the full soundness result but only soundness wrt. a class of derivations that we introduce now:

Definition A.28.

Given a truncation
\tau
, a
\tau
-adapted
\mu\mathsf{MALL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
derivation
\pi
is a
\mu\mathsf{MALL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
pre-proof such that
(i) for all
(\varphi\binampersand\psi)_{\alpha}
occurring in
\pi
, either
\alpha\in Dom(\tau)
or
\{\alpha\mathtt{l},\alpha\mathtt{r}\}\cup\tau^{-1}(\top)\neq\emptyset
.
(ii) given a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
occurring in
\pi
of conclusion sequent
s
and principal formula
(\varphi_{1}\binampersand\varphi_{2})_{\alpha}
, if
\alpha\mathtt{l}\not\in Dom(\tau)
(resp.
\alpha\mathtt{r}\not\in Dom(\tau)
) there is
no
b
-path starting down from the
(\varphi_{1}\binampersand\varphi_{2})_{\alpha}
occurrence of
s
ending in a

(\varphi^{\perp}_{1}\oplus\varphi^{\perp}_{2})_{\beta}
which principal formula of a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}}
inference with
i\neq j
.

We will use soundness for
\tau
-adapted proofs:

Proposition A.29.

Given a truncation
\tau
and a valid
\tau
-adapted
\mu\mathsf{MALL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
derivation
\pi
of conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
, there exists a formula
F\in\Gamma
such that
\llbracket F\rrbracket=\top
.

Proof.

The soundness proof for
\mu\mathsf{MLL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
of proposition
A.12
can be extended to this setting:
first notice that the relation
\leq
on pointed sequents that is used to transfer the marking through
\epsilon
-path can be extended and we will use it in the following consistantly with the underlying slice
\tau
-adaptation suggests.

Construction of
(s_{i})
shall now treat the additive inferences: (i) for the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus)}}
rule, nothing is to be changed since the rule is unary:
s_{i+1}
is the premiss of
s_{i}
; (ii) for the
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
inference on
s_{i}
of principal occurrence
G=H\binampersand K
, we reason as in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
: since
[f_{i}(G)]=\mathbf{0}
and
f_{i}(G)
is of the form
H_{m}\binampersand K_{m}
where
H_{m}
and
K_{m}
are marking of
H
and
K
respectively, then either
[H_{m}]=\mathbf{0}
or
[K_{m}]=\mathbf{0}
.
Moreover, by
\tau
-adaptation, we know that the address of one of
H,K
is in
\tau^{-1}(\top)
and therefore we necessarily choose the other disjunct suppose w.l.o.g. that
[H_{m}]=\mathbf{0}
. We set
s_{i+1}
to be the
premiss of
s_{i}
that contains
H
.

The definition of the sequence of markings
(f_{i})
is trivially extended (more precisely the clauses for the
\binampersand
and
\oplus
are those used in
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
.

The multiplicative soundness argument can be carried over in this setting since by
\tau
-adaptation, the branch we have built is part of the persistent valid slice induced by
\tau
(that is the purpose for the
\tau
-adaptation requirement) and we therefore have a thread for which the decreasing sequence of ordinal can be applied concluding soundness for those derivations.
∎

We can finally establish productivity of cut-elimination:

sketch.

Let
\pi
be a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
valid proof.
By contradiction, assume that
\pi
has a fair infinite sequence of internal reductions.

Wlog.
we can assume that all reductions steps from
\pi
are internal.

For each
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
inference of
\pi
, the trace of this cut-elimination contains at most one premiss
ie.
it is contained in a slice: one can actually find a persistent slice
S
which contains the trace.

The previous remark ensures that for each
\binampersand
formula principal in the trace, the truncation
\tau
of the reduction path contains one of its subformulas in its domain and that the truncated proof
\pi_{\tau}
associated with the trace is
\tau
-adapted.

Since the reduction contains only internal reductions, the conclusion formulas of
\pi_{\tau}
are never principal in the
\pi_{\tau}
and therefore we can erase them resulting in
\pi^{\prime}_{\tau}
which is a
\tau
-adapted
\mu\mathsf{MALL^{\infty}_{\tau}}
valid derivation of the empty sequent which cannot be by soundness (prop
A.29
).
∎

Theorem A.30.

Given
\pi
a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
proof, any fair mcut-reduction from
\pi
produces
a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}^{\infty}
proof.

Sketch.

Let
\pi
be a
\mu\mathsf{MALL}_{\mathsf{m}}^{\infty}
proof of conclusion
\vdash F
and
\pi^{\prime}
the cut-free preproof resulting from the previous property.

By contradiction, assume
\pi^{\prime}
is non-valid. That means there exists a slice
S^{\prime}
of
\pi^{\prime}
(
\pi^{\prime}
is cut-free so there is no persistency assumption applying here) and an infinite branch
\beta^{\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
such that
\beta^{\prime}
is supported by no valid thread.
By the pull-back property,
S^{\prime}
has been built by reducing a persistent slice
S
of
\pi
and therefore
\beta^{\prime}
corresponds to a reduction path from
S
.
Since we are working in a persistent additive slice, the multiplicative validity can be extended in order to extract an infinite branch
\beta
invalid in
S
.
The construction of the proof
\beta^{\prime\perp}
used to obtain the contradiction is lifted to the additive case as follows:

•

first one shall define truncation
\tau^{\prime}
not only by considering the occurrences of
F_{1}\bindnasrepma F_{2}
which are principal in
\beta^{\prime}
, but also those of
F_{1}\oplus F_{2}
and extend the truncation to
\tau^{\prime}(F_{i})=0
if
F_{1}\oplus F_{2}
is the principal occurrence of a
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\oplus_{j})}}
rule with
i\neq j
.

•

then the construction of the dual of branch
\beta
,
\beta^{\perp}
, is extended with the following clauses to the definition of page
•
‣
A.5.1
:

–

F=G\binampersand H
. Suppose
wlog.
that
G\in s_{i+1}
(i.e.,
H
left the branch
\beta
).

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=390.31316pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\oplus_{1})}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.64629pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=107.54366pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime},G)$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

–

F=G\oplus H
and
G\in\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and
H\notin\mathtt{Dom}(\tau^{\prime})
. We set:

\beta_{\geq i}^{\bot}(\Delta)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=540.14583pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\binampersand)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 149.85419pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\kern 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=455.06862pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 5.62477pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\moveright 22.66733pt\hbox{}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hrule width=45.33467pt,height=0.0pt\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 3.0pt}}$}\kern 5.0pt\hbox{{\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\tau^{\prime})}$}, {\scriptsize$\mathsf{(\top)}$}}}}\hrule height=0.0pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},G^{\bot}$}}}}\hbox{$\beta_{\geq i+1}^{\bot}(\Delta^{\prime},H)$}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},H^{\bot}$}}}}}}\hbox{$\vdash\Delta^{\prime\bot},F^{\bot}$}}}}}}$}

\beta^{\prime\perp}
is cut-free and valid (therefore valid in the sense of
(
Baelde
et al. 2016
)
) since its threads are dual of the threads of
\beta
(which are invalid).

\beta^{\prime\perp}
is
\tau^{\prime}
-adapted (as
\beta^{\perp}
is cut-free, this amounts to checking that the appropriate premiss of each
{\scriptsize\mathsf{(\binampersand)}}
has its formula in the domain of
\tau^{\prime}
which is by design) and
by proposition
A.29
, we conclude the desired contradiction since one the one hand we have
\llbracket F\rrbracket=\top
and on the other hand we have
\llbracket F^{\perp}\rrbracket=\top
.
∎

A.7.
(Un)decidability properties

In this appendix, we prove the undecidability of the general bouncing criterion and introduce a hierarchy of decidable sub-criteria.

Proof of decidability for the bounded height criterion

We start by detailing some structure of proofs, via the notion of
shortcut
.

Definition A.31.

A
shortcut
is a finite pre-thread
t=uv
where
\mathsf{w}(u)\in W^{*}A
and
v
is a b-path.
A shortcut
t
is
minimal
if no strict prefix of
t
is a shortcut.

Notice that if
t
is a shortcut, then
t
is an
\epsilon
-path.

We will note
(F,s)
a pointed sequent:
s
is a sequent of the proof, and
F\in s
.
We want to be able to follow threads where shortcuts have been removed. These threads behave like straight threads, except on cuts where they allowed to jump from the starting point of a shortcut to its end.
We will now formalize a description of such “jumping” threads.

Let
\Sigma_{\textit{jump}}=\{\mathsf{W},\mathtt{i},\mathtt{l},\mathtt{r},c_{l},c_{r}\}
, where
c_{l}
(resp.
c_{r}
) stands for left (resp. right) cut occurrences.
A word
\tau\in\Sigma_{\textit{jump}}^{*}
will be called a
relative address
.
If
P
is a preproof and
(F,s)
is a pointed sequent of
P
, then a relative address
\tau
points to another pointed sequent
\tau@(F,s)
in
P
. We define this by induction on
\tau
:

•

If
\tau=\epsilon
then
\tau@(F,s)=(F,s)
.

•

If
\tau=\mathsf{W}\tau^{\prime}
then
\tau@(F,s)=\tau^{\prime}@(F,s^{\prime})
, where
s^{\prime}
is the premiss of
s
containing
F
.

•

If
\tau=\mathtt{i}\tau^{\prime}
,
F=\sigma X.G
(for some
\sigma\in\{\mu,\nu\}
) is principal in
s
with premiss
s^{\prime}
, then
\tau@F=\tau^{\prime}@(G[F/X],s^{\prime})
.

•

If
\tau=\mathtt{l}\tau^{\prime}
(resp.
\tau=\mathtt{r}\tau^{\prime})
,
F=G\star H
(for some

\star\in\{\bindnasrepma,{\otimes}\}
) is principal in
s
, then
\tau@(F,s)=\tau^{\prime}@(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})
where
F^{\prime}=G
(resp.
F^{\prime}=H
) and
s^{\prime}
is the premiss of
s
containing
F^{\prime}
.

•

If
\tau=c_{l}\tau^{\prime}
(resp.
\tau=c_{r}\tau^{\prime}
), and the rule applied to
s
in
P
is a cut, then
\tau@(F,s)=\tau^{\prime}@(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})
, where
F^{\prime}
is the occurrence introduced by the cut on the left (resp. right) premiss
s^{\prime}

of this cut.

•

Otherwise,
\tau@(F,s)
is undefined.

Lemma A.32.

Let
P
be a circular pre-proof. If
t
is a minimal shortcut from
(F,s)
to
(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})
, then
F\equiv F^{\prime}
. Moreover, there is a relative address
\tau
such that
(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\tau@(F,s)
. This
\tau
is called the
effect
of
t
and noted
\mathit{effect}(t)
.
For each pointed sequent
(F,s)
, there is at most one minimal shortcut starting in
(F,s)

Proof.

Since the weight of minimal shortcut starts with
W^{*}A
, no choice is possible before an axiom is encountered. When going downwards, constraints will be pushed on the constraint stack, and the shortcut is again uniquely defined. When going upwards (i.e. after having seen a cut, and before the next axiom), two cases can occur. Either the constraint stack is not empty, and therefore it uniquely determines the path followed by the shortcut, or it is empty, which marks the end of the minimal shortcut
t
.
The relative address
\tau
is given by the position of the end of
t
relatively to the beginning of
t
in the proof tree.
The fact that
F\equiv F^{\prime}
follows from the fact that any shortcut is an
\epsilon
-path.
∎

If
P
is a pre-proof and
(F,s)
is a pointed sequent in
P
, we note
\mathit{short}(F,s)
the minimal shortcut starting in
(F,s)
if it exists. If not, we fix
\mathit{short}(F,s):=\epsilon
. We also fix
\mathit{effect}(\epsilon)=\epsilon
.
We will abbreviate
\mathit{effect}(\mathit{short}(F,s))
by
\mathit{effect}(F,s)
to lighten notations.

Remark 2.

If
P
is a circular pre-proof, and
(F,s),(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})
are two pointed sequents of
P
corresponding to the same occurrence in the finite graph of
P
, then
\mathit{effect}(F,s)=\mathit{effect}(F^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.

This remark allows us to compute only finitely many effects: one for each pointed sequent in the finite proof graph.

Definition A.33.

An
s-thread
is a sequence
(F_{i},s_{i},\uparrow)_{i\in\omega}
that obeys the same rules as a thread going only upwards, with some relaxation in the constraints between
(F_{i},s_{i},\uparrow)
and
(F_{i+1},s_{i+1},\uparrow)
defining a pre-thread.
Indeed we add a new clause allowing the s-thread to take minimal shortcuts:

(F_{i+1},s_{i+1})
can be reached from
(F_{i},s_{i})
following the relative address
\mathit{effect}(F_{i},s_{i})
, i.e.
(F_{i+1},s_{i+1})=\mathit{effect}(F_{i},s_{i})@(F_{i},s_{i})
.

The weight of an s-thread is defined by generalizing the definition of weight of a thread, matching this new clause with
w_{i}=\mathsf{W}
. The notion of visible part and validity of an s-thread is then induced by this definition.

Notice that the visible part of an s-thread is obtained by simply removing steps introduced by this new clause, corresponding to shortcuts.

Lemma A.34.

An infinite branch is validated by a thread if and only if it is validated by an s-thread.

Proof.

The s-thread is obtained from the thread by compressing minimal shortcuts and replacing them with the new clause.
Conversely, the thread can be obtained from the s-thread by replacing the new clause with minimal shortcuts.
This transformation preserves the visible part.
∎

We now give the proof of Theorem
6.4
, stating that any valid proof of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
is a
k
-proof for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let
P
be a valid proof of
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
.
Each pointed sequent in
P
can be annotated with its effect, and with the height of its minimal shortcut (or with
(\epsilon,0)
if this minimal shortcut does not exist).

By Lemma
A.34
, all infinite branches of
P
are validated by s-threads, following effects annotating
P
.
Let
k
be the maximal height annotating a pointed sequent in
P
. We obtain that
P
is a
k
-proof.
∎

The rest of the section is devoted to proving Theorem
6.5
, stating that given a pre-proof
P
and an integer
k
, it is decidable whether
P
is a
k
-proof.

Proof.

Let us note

\mathit{effect}_{k}(F,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathit{effect}(F,s)&\text{if $\mathit{short}(F,s)$ has height $\leq k$}\\
\epsilon&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

For each
(F,s)
in the graph,
\mathit{effect}_{k}(F,s)
can be computed in a finite time.
Indeed, it suffices to follow the only possible thread starting in
(F,s)
in the graph of
P
, until we find a minimal shortcut or we detect a failure. Reasons for failure are:

•

The weight does not begin with
\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathsf{A}
, i.e. an unfolding happen before the first axiom,

•

the constraint stack gets higher than
k
,

•

we detect a loop: the same pointed sequent is visited twice with identical stack content.

This corresponds to turning the automaton
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{thread}}
into a DFA, by bounding the size of the stack to
k
, and accept words of the form
\mathsf{W}^{*}\mathsf{A}\Sigma^{*}C
by empty stack.
This allows us to annotate each pointed sequent
(F,s)
with
\mathit{effect}_{k}(F,s)
.

We can now verify that the pre-proof is a
k
-proof, using a nondeterministic parity automaton
\mathcal{A}_{s}
reading branches of
P
and guessing the existence of an s-thread. This automaton is identical to the one in
(
Doumane 2017
)
for straight threads, except that it can follow effects. While following the relative address given by an effect, the thread is considered hidden, and the action preformed on it does not influence the accepting condition of
\mathcal{A}_{s}
. Since the length of effects is globally bounded (there are finitely many of them), the relative adresses to follow can be stored in the state space of the automaton. The pre-proof
P
is a
k
-proof if and only if
\mathcal{A}_{s}
accepts all branches.
∎

A.7.1.
Details on the undecidability proof

We show that the validity condition is already undecidable for the proof system
\mu\mathsf{MLL}^{\omega}
.

We reduce from the halting problem for two-counter machines (2CM), known to be
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete
(
Minsky 1961
)
.

Here is a brief outline of the proof.

We start by recalling the definition of 2CM in the next section. These are finite-state deterministic machines manipulating two counters, able to perform Zero test, increment and decrement on each counter.

We then show how to encode the halting problem of a 2CM
M
using bouncing threads. The idea is to use the constraint stack to encode the value of counters, and position in the graph to encode the control state. Gadgets allow to increment or decrement each counter.
The main difficulty lies in the tests performed by the machine: we want to design a conditional branching on the thread, depending on the value of the constraint stack. This can be done, but because of the linearity of the proof system, we cannot avoid leaving some extra constraints encoding the results of the tests, that will be collected by the thread later.
Since we want to finish with empty constraint, we need to erase this extra information.
To do this, we add a second gadget performing the computation in a dual way: results of tests are fed to the thread, that rewinds the computation while erasing these extra constraints.
We can finally exit the detour with (almost) no constraints, and perform a visible
\nu
-unfolding on the main branch, before looping back to the root of the proof.

The global pre-proof will be a valid proof according to the criterion if and only if the machine
M
halts.

Two Counter Machines

A 2CM
M
is a tuple
(Q,q_{0},q_{f},\delta)
where
Q
is a finite set of states,
q_{0}
is the initial state,
q_{f}
is the final state, and
\delta
is the transition function.
The machine has access to two counters storing nonnegative integer values. The counters are initialized to
0
.

The possible actions of the machine are the following, where
\tau\in\{1,2\}
identifies one of the counters:

•

\mathrm{Inc}_{\tau}(q)
: increment counter
\tau
, and jump to state
q

•

\mathrm{Dec}_{\tau}(q)
: decrement counter
\tau
, and jump to state
q

•

\mathrm{Test}_{\tau}(q_{Z},q_{P})
: if the current value of counter
\tau
is
0
, jump to
q_{Z}
, else jump to
q_{P}
.

Let
\mathrm{Act}=\{Inc_{\tau}(q)\mid\tau\in\{1,2\},q\in Q\}\cup\{Dec_{\tau}(q)\mid\tau\in\{1,2\},q\in Q\}\cup\{\mathrm{Test}_{\tau}(q_{Z},q_{P})\mid\tau\in\{1,2\},q_{Z},q_{P}\in Q\}
be the set of possible actions.

The transition function of
M
is a function
\delta:Q\setminus\{q_{f}\}\to\mathrm{Act}
, specifying which action is executed when each state is reached. No action is mapped to
q_{f}
, since the run stops when
q_{f}
is reached.

A
configuration
of the machine
M
is a triple
(p,k[1],k[2])\in Q\times\mathbb{N}^{2}
specifying the current state and the values for the two counters.

A
run
of the machine
M
is a sequence of configurations
(p_{i},k[1]_{i},k[2]_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n}
such that
p_{0}=q_{0}
,
k[1]_{0}=k[2]_{0}=0
,
p_{n}=q_{f}
, and consistent with
\delta
, i.e. for all
i\in[0,n-1]:
, we have

•

if
\delta(p_{i})=\mathrm{Inc}_{\tau}(q)
then
p_{i+1}=q
and
k[\tau]_{i+1}=k[\tau]_{i}+1
.

•

if
\delta(p_{i})=\mathrm{Dec}_{\tau}(q)
then
p_{i+1}=q
and
k[\tau]_{i+1}=k[\tau]_{i}-1
.

•

if
\delta(p_{i})=\mathrm{Test}_{\tau}(q_{Z},q_{P})
, then
k[\tau]_{i+1}=k[\tau]_{i}
, and

–

if
k[\tau]_{i}=0
then
p_{i+1}=q_{Z}
.

–

if
k[\tau]_{i}>0
then
p_{i+1}=q_{P}
.

In all cases the other counter is left unchanged, i.e.
k[3-\tau]_{i+1}=k[3-\tau]_{i}

Without loss of generality, we can also assume that the run ends with both counter values equal to
0
, i.e.
k[1]_{n}=k[2]_{n}=0
.

The next theorem states that the halting problem is undecidable for Two Counter Machines.

Theorem A.35.

(
Minsky 1961
)

Given a Two Counter Machine
M=(Q,q_{0},q_{f},\delta)
, it is undecidable to determine whether
M
has a run, by a reduction from Turing Machines halting problem.

From machines to proofs

We will now encode the halting problem for 2CM into the problem of deciding whether a preproof is a proof.

We fix a machine
M=(Q,q_{0},q_{f},\delta)
.

We will build a preproof
P
such that the leftmost infinite branch can be validated by a bouncing thread if and only if there exists a run of the machine
M
. All the other branches of
P
will be validated by non-bouncing threads.

We will use throughout the proof the formulas
F,G
, where
F=\nu X.(X\bindnasrepma X)
,
G=F^{\perp}=\mu X.(X{\otimes}X)
, and auxiliary formulas
A=\nu X.(X\bindnasrepma X){\otimes}X
and
B=\mu X.(X\bindnasrepma A\bindnasrepma A)
. Their addresses will sometimes be omitted, keep in mind that a letter can represent different occurrences in a proof tree.

The thread will always follow a formula
F
when going upwards, and
G
when going downwards. The formula
A
will be used to ensure that all infinite branches except the leftmost one are validated by non-bouncing threads.

The conclusion of the proof
P
is the sequent
G,F,B
.

The idea of the construction is to use a bouncing thread to encode a run of
M
, by storing the current configuration on
M
in the stack of constraints that the thread must satisfy.

The general shape of the preproof
P
is given in Figure
12
. By convention, formulas introduced in cuts will always be
F
on the left and
G
on the right. This means that a thread going upwards following a formula
F
will always turn right on cuts, bounce on axioms in the right part, and finally come back to visit the left part of the cut.

\mathsf{(\mu,\bindnasrepma)}

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{(Cut)}

\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mu},\nu,\bindnasrepma,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\otimes}})}

\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mu},\nu,\bindnasrepma,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\otimes}})}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\mathsf{(Ax)}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G_{\mathbf{l}},F_{\mathbf{l}}}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G_{\mathbf{r}\mathbf{r}},F_{\mathbf{r}\mathbf{r}}}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}B}

\pi_{R}
\vdash G,F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}B},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

\pi_{M}
\vdash G,F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G},F,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}B},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}A}
